OpenRemote alarm-ID enumeration via repeated DELETE probing of /api/{realm}/alarm AIReconnaissance Medium
Hunts the enumeration stage of the removeAlarms IDOR: a single user or source IP firing many DELETE requests at the OpenRemote alarm endpoint with sequential auto-increment IDs, distinguishing existing vs non-existent alarms by 200 vs 404 responses. Article-specific because it keys on the exact DELETE /api/{realm}/alarm route and the sequential-ID probing behaviour described in the PoC. Rationale: Uses the exact DELETE /api/{realm}/alarm route and the article's 404-vs-200 presence-oracle behaviour over sequential IDs; a burst of DELETEs with mixed status codes from one principal is the enumeration fingerprint. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-h3m5-97jq-qjrf
ATT&CKT1190 T1087
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Automated integration/test suite exercising the alarm API against a staging realm
  • OpenRemote admin bulk-clearing stale alarms during maintenance
  • Monitoring/synthetic checks that create-and-delete alarms in a loop
  • Load-testing tooling replaying DELETE traffic
  • A legitimate alarm-retention job pruning many IDs at once
Suppression filter
| where csUsername != "-" and isnotempty(csUsername) and cIP !in ("127.0.0.1","::1")
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Pull the csUsername/cIP and confirm the account's home realm; check whether the deleted IDs span multiple realms (cross-tenant = confirmed exploit). Step 2 (contain): Disable the offending account/API token and block the source IP at the WAF; force OpenRemote upgrade to >= 1.24.2. Step 3 (hunt): Enumerate all DELETE /*/alarm requests from that principal over 30d, identify which victim realms lost alarms, and restore alarm records from backup where safety/security alerts were destroyed.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where csMethod == "DELETE"
| where csUriStem endswith "/alarm"
| extend realm = extract(@"/api/([^/]+)/alarm", 1, csUriStem)
| summarize ReqCount = count(), StatusVariety = dcount(scStatus), Statuses = make_set(scStatus), Realms = make_set(realm), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by cIP, csUsername, bin(TimeGenerated, 10m)
| where ReqCount > 15 and StatusVariety > 1   // 15 = burst threshold; >1 status = 200/404 presence-probing signature
| order by ReqCount desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count as req_count, dc(Web.status) as status_variety, values(Web.status) as statuses from datamodel=Web where Web.http_method=DELETE (Web.url="*/alarm" OR Web.url="*/alarm?*") by Web.src, Web.user, _time span=10m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| where req_count > 15 AND status_variety > 1
| sort - req_count
| tstats summariesonly=false count as req_count, dc(Web.status) as status_variety, values(Web.status) as statuses from datamodel=Web where Web.http_method=DELETE (Web.url="*/alarm" OR Web.url="*/alarm?*") by Web.src, Web.user, _time span=10m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`

OpenRemote bulk alarm deletion — successful DELETE on /api/{realm}/alarm (removeAlarms IDOR) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the destructive action of GHSA-h3m5-97jq-qjrf: an authenticated caller successfully invoking the bulk removeAlarms endpoint (HTTP DELETE on /api/{realm}/alarm) with a 200 response, permanently destroying alarm records. Article-specific because it targets the exact vulnerable route and verb from the PoC and scopes to successful (200) deletions rather than all traffic. Rationale: Keys on the exact vulnerable route (DELETE /api/{realm}/alarm) and successful 200 status from the PoC, giving a direct signal for the destructive removeAlarms call rather than a generic technique template. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-h3m5-97jq-qjrf
ATT&CKT1485 T1190

Suppression filter

Response runbook

Step 2 (contain): Revoke the account's session/API token, patch OpenRemote to >= 1.24.2, and enable request-body logging on the alarm endpoint. Step 3 (hunt): Review all successful DELETEs on /*/alarm for the past 30d, map destroyed alarm IDs to victim realms, and prioritise restoring safety-critical/security alarms from backup.

OpenRemote cross-realm alarm deletion — user deletes alarms in a realm outside their baseline (IDOR priv-esc) AIExploitation Medium
Detects the core privilege-escalation step of the IDOR: an authenticated user issuing a successful DELETE against /api/{realm}/alarm for a realm they have never legitimately operated in. Builds a per-user baseline of accessed realms and flags first-time cross-realm deletions — directly modelling the article's 'authenticated user in any realm can delete alarms belonging to other realms'. Rationale: Extracts the realm from the article's /api/{realm}/alarm path and anti-joins each successful DELETE against the user's historically-accessed realms — a first-time cross-realm deletion is exactly the cross-tenant IDOR the advisory describes. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-h3m5-97jq-qjrf
ATT&CKT1548 T1190
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A genuine multi-realm administrator whose 14d baseline didn't yet include a rarely-used realm
  • New realm onboarded during the window so no baseline exists yet
  • Superuser/service account that legitimately spans all realms
  • Username casing/format differences causing baseline mismatch
  • Shared load-balancer or proxy collapsing csUsername to a service identity
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): Confirm the flagged user's provisioned realm membership in OpenRemote's identity/ACL config; if the victim realm isn't assigned to them, the IDOR is confirmed. Step 2 (contain): Suspend the account and its tokens, upgrade to >= 1.24.2, and notify the victim tenant(s) that alarm records were destroyed. Step 3 (hunt): Pivot on the source IP and any other accounts it authenticated, and enumerate every cross-realm DELETE to size the blast radius across tenants.
let lookback = 14d;
let baseline =
    W3CIISLog
    | where TimeGenerated between (ago(lookback) .. ago(1d))
    | where csUriStem startswith "/api/"
    | extend baseline_realm = extract(@"/api/([^/]+)/", 1, csUriStem)
    | where isnotempty(baseline_realm) and isnotempty(csUsername) and csUsername != "-"
    | summarize by csUsername, baseline_realm;

| summarize DelCount = count(), VictimRealms = make_set(realm), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), SrcIPs = make_set(cIP) by csUsername
| order by DelCount desc

Rationale: Combines the article's DELETE /api/{realm}/alarm route with a service-account name filter to catch stolen-credential abuse of the IDOR, since the vuln requires only authentication and service accounts performing bulk alarm deletes is anomalous. Langflow ingestion process reads OS credential files (shadow/ssh/aws) via TAR symlink AIExploitation High
Hunts for the Langflow web/worker process (python/uvicorn/gunicorn/langflow) opening high-value OS secret files it never legitimately touches — /etc/shadow, ~/.ssh private keys, ~/.aws/credentials — the payoff of the CVE-2026-55447 BaseFileComponent symlink-in-TAR arbitrary file read. A document-ingestion node reading these paths is the core exploit signal, independent of the specific component (Read File, Docling, Unstructured, etc.). Rationale: Uses the exact post-exploitation targets named in the advisory/IBM bulletin (JWT secret, and by extension /etc/shadow, ssh, aws creds a symlink can reach) scoped to the Langflow runtime processes; a RAG file-parser touching these paths has no benign explanation. Cross-checked GitLab advisory and IBM security bulletin. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/langflow/CVE-2026-55447/ • https://www.ibm.com/support/pages/security-bulletin-path-traversal-vulnerability-file-processing-components-allows-unauthorized-file-system-access-and-potential-remote-code-execution
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1083
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Backup/agent software (restic, borg) run under the same service account reading ~/.ssh or ~/.aws for its own auth
  • Admin debugging the container as the langflow user and catting /etc/passwd
  • Config-management (Ansible) module invoked in-process touching authorized_keys
  • Security scanner (Wazuh/osquery FIM) reading /etc/shadow under an unrelated python wrapper misattributed to the app account
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !in~ ("root") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("gunicorn","uvicorn","langflow run")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the reading PID is the Langflow app (uvicorn/gunicorn worker) and pull the ingestion request/upload that immediately preceded the read; inspect the uploaded TAR for symlink members. 2) Contain: revoke/rotate any secret exposed (LANGFLOW_SECRET_KEY, RS256 private_key.pem, SSH keys, AWS keys), invalidate active Langflow JWTs, and take the flow offline. 3) Hunt: check for subsequent JWT-forged admin logins, new flows containing a Python Interpreter node, and child shell/network processes off the Langflow worker.

Langflow secret_key / RS256 private key / config file access (JWT secret theft) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts access to Langflow's own secret material — the HS256 secret_key file, RS256 private_key.pem, and config dir under ~/.langflow — which the CVE-2026-55447 chain steals to forge JWTs and bypass authentication. Focuses on the file that turns arbitrary read into full auth bypass and RCE. Rationale: The advisory explicitly names Langflow's secret_key JWT file as the exploit target; corroborating Langflow docs confirm HS256 secret_key and RS256 private_key.pem/public_key.pem live in LANGFLOW_CONFIG_DIR (~/.langflow, /app/langflow). Medium confidence because Langflow legitimately reads its own secret_key at startup — see FP filter. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-ccv6-r384-xp75 • https://docs.langflow.org/jwt-authentication
ATT&CKT1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Langflow's own startup read of secret_key when the process (re)launches — benign and frequent
  • RS256 key-pair auto-generation on first boot writing private_key.pem/public_key.pem
  • Backup job snapshotting the ~/.langflow config directory
  • Container image build/CI reading config.json for a health check
Suppression filter
| where not (Reads <= 2 and FileName =~ "secret_key" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("langflow run","gunicorn","uvicorn") and FolderPath has_any ("/.langflow/","/app/langflow"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: correlate the secret_key read against an inbound file-upload/ingestion event within seconds — a read tied to document ingestion (not process start) is the true positive. 2) Contain: rotate LANGFLOW_SECRET_KEY / RS256 key pair and force re-auth of all users, upgrade Langflow to >=1.9.2. 3) Hunt: search for admin-privileged API calls, flow creation, or Python Interpreter nodes appearing shortly after the read.

Symlink member written to Langflow TAR extraction / temp dir (traversal mechanism) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the mechanism itself: BaseFileComponent._unpack_bundle extracting a TAR whose members are symlinks pointing outside the extract directory. Detects a Langflow process creating symlink files in temp/extract directories (/tmp, /var/tmp, ~/.cache/langflow) whose targets reference sensitive paths — the pre-condition to the arbitrary read. Rationale: Directly targets the advisory's root cause — _unpack_bundle extracting TAR symlink members into the extract dir. Symlink-specific telemetry requires Sysmon-for-Linux/auditd symlink-syscall auditing, hence Medium confidence; naming-token filters keep it high-signal where that telemetry exists. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-ccv6-r384-xp75
ATT&CKT1083 T1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legit RAG uploads that happen to contain benign symlinks in an archive
  • Python package installs (pip) creating symlinks under a temp build dir
  • App startup extracting bundled sample data to ~/.cache/langflow
  • Backup/restore tooling recreating symlinked trees in /tmp
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the symlink's target path and the preceding upload; a symlink pointing to /etc/shadow, ssh keys, or secret_key is a confirmed exploit attempt. 2) Contain: quarantine the uploaded archive, block the uploading user/API key, and patch to Langflow >=1.9.2 which rejects non-regular TAR members. 3) Hunt: pivot to the file-read (UC #1/#2) and any post-read egress or new flows on the same host.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("python","python3","uvicorn","gunicorn","langflow")
| where ActionType == "FileCreated"
| where FolderPath has_any ("/tmp/","/var/tmp/","/.cache/langflow","/app/langflow")
| where AdditionalFields has "symlink" or FileName in~ ("secret_key","id_rsa","credentials","private_key.pem","shadow")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, AdditionalFields, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where Facility in ("auth","authpriv","kern","user") or ProcessName in ("python","python3","uvicorn","gunicorn","langflow")
| where SyslogMessage has_any ("symlink","symlinkat")
| where SyslogMessage has_any ("/tmp/","/var/tmp/",".cache/langflow") and SyslogMessage has_any ("secret_key","id_rsa",".aws/credentials","private_key.pem","/etc/shadow")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Langflow Python Interpreter node RCE — worker spawns shell/download child process AIInstallation High
Hunts the final CVE-2026-55447 stage: after forging a JWT, the attacker creates a flow with a 'Python Interpreter' node and executes arbitrary code, causing the Langflow worker (python/uvicorn/gunicorn) to spawn shell or network-download child processes it normally never launches. Rationale: The advisory states the second stage is a 'Python Interpreter' node executing arbitrary Python for RCE; a web-app worker spawning sh/bash/curl/wget is the observable host artifact of that node, with near-zero benign baseline for a Langflow service. High-fidelity parent→child pair. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-ccv6-r384-xp75 • https://news.kenbuckler.com/2026/06/exploit-alert-langflow.html
ATT&CKT1059.006 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A flow legitimately using a shell/command component or subprocess for a supported integration
  • Langflow health-check or entrypoint script invoking curl/wget for readiness probes
  • Ops running a maintenance one-liner as the langflow user
  • A custom component that shells out to pandoc/ffmpeg via bash
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("http://","https://","-e ","/dev/tcp/","bash -i","python -c","base64 -d") or FileName in~ ("nc","ncat","socat")
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the child ProcessCommandLine and the flow/user that triggered it; a curl/wget to an external host or a reverse-shell one-liner is a confirmed RCE. 2) Contain: kill the worker, isolate the host, revoke Langflow JWTs and rotate LANGFLOW_SECRET_KEY, upgrade to >=1.9.2. 3) Hunt: enumerate all flows for Python Interpreter/shell nodes, review outbound connections from the host, and check for new persistence (cron, systemd, authorized_keys writes).

Langflow credential-file read followed by outbound egress within 60s (exfiltration) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Correlates a sensitive-file read by the Langflow worker (secret_key, ssh/aws creds, /etc/shadow) with an outbound public network connection from the same host and process within 60 seconds — the exfiltration tail of the CVE-2026-55447 chain, whether via a Python Interpreter node or direct data-out. Rationale: Chains the advisory's arbitrary-read targets to an egress from the same Langflow process/host inside a tight 60s window — the read-then-send pattern implied by the exfiltration/RCE stages. Correlation (no single-event Sigma) keeps FP low; Medium confidence because benign app egress exists. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-ccv6-r384-xp75 • https://www.ibm.com/support/pages/security-bulletin-path-traversal-vulnerability-file-processing-components-allows-unauthorized-file-system-access-and-potential-remote-code-execution
ATT&CKT1041 T1020
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Langflow making a legitimate LLM API call (OpenAI/Anthropic) shortly after reading its own secret_key for decryption
  • Telemetry/observability agent (Langfuse) posting traces right after a config read
  • Backup agent reading creds then uploading to cloud storage
  • Package/model download coinciding with a config-dir read
Suppression filter
| where RemoteUrl !has_any ("openai.com","anthropic.com","cohere.ai","huggingface.co","langfuse.com") or RemotePort !in (443,80)
Response runbook
1) Triage: resolve the RemoteIP/RemoteUrl — a known LLM/API endpoint is likely benign; an unfamiliar IP, raw-IP, or non-standard port after reading /etc/shadow or ssh keys is exfiltration. 2) Contain: block the destination, isolate the host, rotate every credential the read touched, and invalidate Langflow JWTs. 3) Hunt: decode any base64/POST body in proxy logs, review the flow that triggered it, and check other hosts running the vulnerable Langflow (<1.9.2).

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Craft CMS resource-js Host/X-Forwarded-Host poisoning (CVE-2026-55791) · [LLM] Craft CMS trustedHosts bypass via localhost/127.0.0.1 Host header from external IP
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Blind SSRF: Craft web-server process fetching internal/cloud-metadata hosts
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Craft CMS 1-click RCE: plugin-install POST riding a poisoned resource-js JS load
Craft CMS resource-js Host/X-Forwarded-Host poisoning (CVE-2026-55791) AIDelivery Medium
Hunts unauthenticated requests to Craft's /actions/app/resource-js endpoint where a single client cycles multiple distinct Host header values, the fingerprint of trustedHosts:['any'] abuse poisoning $baseUrl. Article-specific: only this endpoint reflects attacker-controlled content as application/javascript. Rationale: Anchored on the exact vulnerable URI 'app/resource-js' and the article's stated mechanism of poisoning the Host header to manipulate $baseUrl; dcount(Host) per client avoids needing the victim's domain (no placeholder) while flagging the header-rewriting pattern. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh/
ATT&CKT1190 T1071.001
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate multi-domain Craft install serving several front-end hostnames from one CDN/proxy egress IP
  • Uptime/synthetic monitors probing the site with varied Host headers
  • Security scanners (Burp, Nuclei) run by the org's own pentest team
  • Shared NAT egress where many real users appear as one SrcIpAddr
  • CDN revalidation requests carrying origin-shield Host values
Suppression filter
| where DstHostname !in ("monitoring.internal")  // append known synthetic-monitor egress hosts after baselining
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull HostSamples for the flagged SrcIpAddr — confirm values are IP literals, internal names, or attacker domains rather than the site's real hostnames; check response status/size on those requests. 2) Contain: in config/general.php set trustedHosts to the explicit allowed host(s) and upgrade craftcms/cms to >=4.17.9 / >=5.9.15; block the source IP at the WAF. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC for outbound fetches from the web-server process and to admin plugin-install POSTs from the same window.
let lookback = 24h;
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where Url has "resource-js"
| extend HostHeader = tostring(HttpHostName)
| where isnotempty(HostHeader)
| summarize DistinctHosts = dcount(HostHeader), HostSamples = make_set(HostHeader, 10), Requests = count(), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by SrcIpAddr, DstHostname
| where DistinctHosts > 3   // one client cycling >3 distinct Host values at resource-js = header fuzzing / baseUrl poisoning
| order by DistinctHosts desc

Craft CMS trustedHosts bypass via localhost/127.0.0.1 Host header from external IP AIDelivery Medium
Detects requests to Craft's resource-js endpoint where the Host or X-Forwarded-Host header claims to be localhost/127.0.0.1/::1 but the source IP is public — a direct attempt to defeat front-end Host validation against the trustedHosts:['any'] default. Single occurrence is high-fidelity. Rationale: Uses the exact vulnerable endpoint plus the concrete loopback literals attackers supply to spoof a trusted origin; pairing a loopback Host with a public source IP is behaviour no legitimate client produces, making it a low-FP alerting candidate. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh/
ATT&CKT1190 T1199
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal health-check that wrongly egresses through a public IP while sending Host: localhost
  • Misconfigured reverse proxy forwarding a literal localhost Host upstream
  • Container-to-container probe traversing a public NAT
  • Pentest/scanner deliberately fuzzing Host headers
  • Load balancer health probe rewritten to 127.0.0.1
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm SrcIpAddr is genuinely external and not an internal monitor NAT'd to a public address; review HTTP status/size of the response. 2) Contain: set explicit trustedHosts in config/general.php, upgrade craftcms/cms to >=4.17.9 / >=5.9.15, and block the source IP. 3) Hunt: check for outbound fetches from php-fpm/nginx to internal or metadata IPs and for any /admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin POSTs in the following minutes.
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(24h)
| where Url has "resource-js"
| extend HostHeader = tolower(tostring(HttpHostName))
| where HostHeader has_any ("localhost","127.0.0.1","[::1]","::1","0.0.0.0")
| where not(ipv4_is_private(SrcIpAddr)) and SrcIpAddr !in ("::1","127.0.0.1")
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, HttpHostName, Url, HttpMethod, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent = HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent, DstHostname
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Blind SSRF: Craft web-server process fetching internal/cloud-metadata hosts AICommand & Control Medium
Detects the SSRF half of CVE-2026-55791 — Craft's Guzzle client (running under php-fpm/nginx/httpd) making outbound connections to RFC1918, link-local, or the 169.254.169.254 cloud-metadata endpoint after $baseUrl poisoning. Article-specific: the vuln forces this Guzzle fetch with ALLOW_REDIRECTS=true. Rationale: Targets the article's stated server-side fetch (Craft::createGuzzleClient()->get($url)) by pivoting on the web-server process making unexpected internal/metadata connections; the 169.254.169.254 focus is corroborated by Craft's related metadata-SSRF CVE-2026-27129, and DB ports are excluded to suppress routine backend traffic. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pkg/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-27129/

  • php-fpm calling an internal microservice/API on an allowed private host (8080/9200)
  • nginx reverse-proxying to an internal app server
  • Legitimate use of an internal package mirror or Composer proxy
  • App-level health checks hitting internal services
  • Sidecar/service-mesh proxies on private IPs
Suppression filter
| where not (RemoteIP startswith "10." and RemotePort == 9200)  // example: drop known internal Elasticsearch; extend with allowed internal services after baselining
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect the RemoteIP/RemoteUrl and the InitiatingProcessId — was 169.254.169.254 or an internal admin port hit, and is there a matching resource-js request in web logs? 2) Contain: rotate any cloud IAM credentials reachable via metadata if 169.254.169.254 was contacted, set explicit trustedHosts, and patch craftcms/cms. 3) Hunt: review web logs for resource-js Host poisoning from the originating window and for outbound connections to attacker-owned public IPs reflecting fetched JS.

Craft CMS 1-click RCE: plugin-install POST riding a poisoned resource-js JS load AIActions on Objectives High
Correlates the cache-poisoning payoff of CVE-2026-55791 — an admin browser that loaded the resource-js response as application/javascript then, within minutes from the same client, POSTs to /admin/actions/plugins/install-plugin (session riding using window.Craft.csrfTokenValue). This is the RCE step, not a generic CSRF check. Rationale: Uses the two concrete article URIs ('resource-js' returning application/javascript, and 'actions/plugins/install-plugin' POST) plus the same-client temporal link the cache-poisoning-to-session-riding chain requires; plugin install is a rare CP action so the correlation is high-fidelity and low-FP. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/CVE-2026-55791/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/craftcms/cms/GHSA-95wr-3f2v-v2wh/
ATT&CKT1185 T1059.007
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin legitimately installing a plugin shortly after a normal Control-Panel page load
  • Automated CI/deploy tooling that installs plugins via the CP from a fixed IP
  • Penetration test reproducing the chain
  • Admin behind same NAT as the resource-js request during routine work
  • Plugin Store updates triggering install-plugin during a CP session
Suppression filter
| where UserAgent !has "CraftDeployBot"  // exclude known CI deploy automation user-agent after confirming it
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the resource-js response served to that client was attacker-controlled (external Host header, unexpected size/hash) versus the legitimate cached asset; identify the admin account tied to the session. 2) Contain: disable/inspect the installed plugin, force-logout and rotate the admin session + CSRF, set explicit trustedHosts, and patch to >=4.17.9 / >=5.9.15. 3) Hunt: review the file system for newly written plugin/PHP files and the web-server process for child processes or outbound C2 following the install.

[GHSA / CRITICAL] GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r: Crossplane: Signature verification TOCTOU allows installing unverified package content via mutable tag

GitHub Security Advisories
2026-06-19 2 use cases 0 techniques 1 kill-chain phase detected

Crossplane: Signature verification TOCTOU allows installing unverified package content via mutable tag

Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
Likely
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Crossplane package installed by mutable tag instead of immutable digest · [LLM] Crossplane ImageConfig signature-verification policy deleted or weakened
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Crossplane package installed by mutable tag instead of immutable digest AIInstallation High
Hunts Kubernetes API audit logs for pkg.crossplane.io Provider/Configuration/Function installs whose spec.package references a version tag rather than an @sha256 digest. This is the exact precondition GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r requires: when a package is pulled by tag, a malicious/compromised OCI registry can serve a correctly-signed image for cosign verification and then swap in unsigned content for the actual pull. Rationale: Built directly on the advisory's stated exploit precondition #2 ('Install packages using tag references rather than digests'). The detection keys on the real Crossplane API (pkg.crossplane.io Provider/Configuration/Function, spec.package) and the digest sentinel '@sha256:' that the fix mandates; absence of the digest is exactly what makes an install TOCTOU-exposed. Corroborated against the GHSA, the GitLab advisory mirror, and Crossplane's ImageConfig/package docs. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/github.com/crossplane/crossplane/v2/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r/ • https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/packages/image-configs/
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate GitOps/Argo/Flux pipelines that pin Crossplane packages to a semantic-version tag (e.g. provider-aws:v1.20.0) rather than a digest — common and benign in many shops
  • Platform-team installs from the org's own trusted internal registry where tag mutability is acceptable
  • Dev/sandbox clusters where engineers iterate on packages by floating tag
  • Initial cluster bootstrap installing first-party Upbound/Crossplane marketplace packages by tag
  • Renovate/Dependabot-style bots bumping a package tag during routine upgrades
Suppression filter
| where not (PackageRef startswith "xpkg.upbound.io/upbound/" or PackageRef startswith "xpkg.crossplane.io/")   // keep third-party / org-uncontrolled registries (advisory precondition #3), drop trusted first-party marketplace
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the offending Provider/Configuration object and its spec.package. Confirm whether the registry host is one the org controls (advisory precondition #3) and whether an ImageConfig with cosign verification matches that image. A tag-pinned package from a third-party registry under cosign verification is the high-risk case. 2) Contain: re-pin the package to the resolved immutable digest (spec.package: repo@sha256:...) and ensure Crossplane is on a fixed build (v2.3.3 / v2.2.3 or later); for vulnerable builds, prefer digest references org-wide until upgraded. 3) Hunt: inventory all pkg.crossplane.io resources still on tags, review the installed package revisions' Verified condition for skipped/incomplete status, and check whether the registry served differing digests for verify vs pull around the install time.
AKSAudit
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where Verb in ("create", "update", "patch")
| extend ObjRef = todynamic(ObjectRef)
| where tostring(ObjRef.apiGroup) == "pkg.crossplane.io"
| where tostring(ObjRef.resource) in ("providers", "configurations", "functions")
| extend ReqObj = todynamic(RequestObject)
| extend PackageRef = tostring(ReqObj.spec.package)
| where isnotempty(PackageRef) and PackageRef !contains "@sha256:"   // not digest-pinned => mutable tag => TOCTOU-exposed
| extend Actor = tostring(todynamic(User).username)
| project TimeGenerated, Actor, Verb, Kind = tostring(ObjRef.resource), ObjectName = tostring(ObjRef.name), PackageRef, UserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

sourcetype="kube:apiserver:audit" objectRef.apiGroup="pkg.crossplane.io" (objectRef.resource="providers" OR objectRef.resource="configurations" OR objectRef.resource="functions") (verb="create" OR verb="update" OR verb="patch")

| sort - lastTime
Crossplane ImageConfig signature-verification policy deleted or weakened AIInstallation Medium
Alerts on delete/update/patch operations against imageconfigs.pkg.crossplane.io in the Kubernetes audit stream. Crossplane enforces cosign signature verification only for images matched by an ImageConfig (spec.verification.cosign); removing or editing that resource silently disables verification, the defense the GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r TOCTOU already partially undermines. Surfaces whether the post-change object still carries a verification block. Rationale: Grounded in the advisory's statement that 'package signature verification [is] configured via the ImageConfig mechanism' and Crossplane docs confirming the real resource (imageconfigs.pkg.crossplane.io, spec.verification.cosign). Detects the defense-evasion precursor — tearing down or weakening the cosign policy — using only verifiable API/resource names (no skipSignatureVerification field exists in the schema, so that reviewer suggestion was dropped as ungrounded). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wfqx-gjrf-g28r • https://docs.crossplane.io/latest/packages/image-configs/ • https://docs.upbound.io/providers/signature-verification
ATT&CKT1562.001
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Platform engineers legitimately rotating cosign authorities/keys inside an ImageConfig during key rollover
  • GitOps reconciliation re-applying an unchanged ImageConfig (server-side apply 'update' with no real diff)
  • Decommissioning an ImageConfig for a registry the org no longer uses
  • Migrating verification config between ImageConfig objects (delete old + create new)
  • Cert-manager or admission-controller status writes touching the resource
Suppression filter
| where Verb in ("delete", "deletecollection") or VerificationSpecPresent == false   // alert on removal/teardown of verification; suppress edits that retain a verification block
Response runbook
1) Triage: diff the ImageConfig before/after the operation — confirm whether spec.verification.cosign authorities were removed, loosened, or the whole object deleted, and identify the actor (human vs service account) and source IP. A delete or removal of the verification block by a non-platform identity is high-confidence malicious. 2) Contain: restore the ImageConfig from source-of-truth/GitOps, and pause new package installs (or require digest pins) until verification is re-established; rotate credentials of the actor if unauthorized. 3) Hunt: list any package revisions installed while the ImageConfig was absent/weakened and check their Verified condition; cross-reference with the mutable-tag install detection to find packages that may have been pulled without enforced verification.
AKSAudit
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| extend ObjRef = todynamic(ObjectRef)
| where tostring(ObjRef.apiGroup) == "pkg.crossplane.io"
| where tostring(ObjRef.resource) == "imageconfigs"
| where Verb in ("delete", "deletecollection", "update", "patch")
| extend ReqObj = todynamic(RequestObject)
| extend VerificationSpecPresent = isnotempty(tostring(ReqObj.spec.verification))
| extend Actor = tostring(todynamic(User).username)
| project TimeGenerated, Actor, Verb, ImageConfigName = tostring(ObjRef.name), UserAgent, VerificationSpecPresent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Anonymous HTTP 200 to DotVVM endpoints (AuthorizeActionFilter bypass exploitation) AIExploitation Low
Hunts successful (HTTP 200) requests to DotVVM-served content (.dothtml views / dotvvm resource routes) that carry no authenticated user, the observable signature of the no-op AuthorizeActionFilter letting unauthenticated callers reach pages it should have rejected with 401/403. Article-specific because it anchors on DotVVM's own request markers rather than a generic protected-path template. Rationale: Anchored on DotVVM's own request markers ('.dothtml', 'dotvvm' route) confirmed via dotvvm.com routing docs, combined with empty csUserName + scStatus 200 — the only behavioural signature the advisory leaves, since the no-op filter manifests as unauthenticated requests succeeding. defender_kql left empty: Microsoft Defender XDR has no IIS HTTP-status/authenticated-user table (DeviceNetworkEvents lacks HTTP status and request user). Cross-checked GitHub Advisory DB and GitLab advisory (CVSS 3.1, CWE-862). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w • https://advisories.gitlab.com/nuget/dotvvm/GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w/ • https://www.dotvvm.com/docs/2.0/pages/basics-routing
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Public, intentionally anonymous DotVVM pages (landing/login/marketing .dothtml views legitimately return 200 to anonymous users)
  • Static DotVVM resource handler fetches (CSS/JS bundled by the framework under dotvvm resource routes)
  • Load-balancer / uptime monitoring probes hitting DotVVM routes anonymously
  • Search-engine crawlers indexing public DotVVM content
  • Pre-auth bootstrap GET of a DotVVM SPA shell before the user signs in
Suppression filter
| where csMethod == "POST" or csUriStem !endswith ".css" and csUriStem !endswith ".js" and csUriStem !has "dotvvmResource"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the target .dothtml route is meant to be protected (was it decorated with AuthorizeActionFilter?) and that the responding app runs a vulnerable DotVVM build (< 4.2.11, 4.3.0-preview01..4.3.14, or 5.0.0-preview01..08). If the page is public by design, close. 2) Contain: deploy the patched DotVVM (4.3.15 / 4.2.11 / 5.0.0-preview09) or swap AuthorizeActionFilter for AuthorizeAttribute per the advisory workaround; front the route with a WAF rule requiring a valid auth cookie. 3) Hunt: pull all anonymous-200 hits to this site for the same cIP set and review whether sensitive data (export/admin) was returned, then check app logs for state-changing POST postbacks executed without a principal.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where scStatus == 200
// DotVVM framework markers: .dothtml views or the dotvvm resource/postback route
| where csUriStem has "dotvvm" or csUriStem endswith ".dothtml" or csUriQuery has "dotvvm"
// no authenticated principal on the request = anonymous reach to a protected-by-design page
| where isempty(csUserName) or csUserName == "-"
| summarize Requests = count(), Methods = make_set(csMethod, 10), Paths = make_set(csUriStem, 20), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by Computer, cIP, sSiteName
| sort by Requests desc

Vulnerable DotVVM framework version present on host (GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w) AIExploitation Low
Exposure hunt that surfaces assets running a DotVVM build affected by the no-op AuthorizeActionFilter: any version < 4.2.11, the 4.3.x line below 4.3.15, or 5.0.0 previews 01-08. Concrete because it encodes the exact affected/patched version boundaries from the advisory. Rationale: Encodes the advisory's exact version boundaries — patched at 4.3.15 / 4.2.11 / 5.0.0-preview09 — with explicit handling of the preview-suffix branch so 5.0.0-preview08 is flagged but preview09 is not. Confidence Low because DotVVM is an embedded NuGet dependency and may not always appear in Defender TVM software inventory; where it does, the match is exact (fp low). Versions cross-checked against the GitHub and GitLab advisory entries. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w • https://advisories.gitlab.com/nuget/dotvvm/GHSA-c8qj-jx8j-fg2w/
ATT&CKT1190
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A DotVVM app that does not use AuthorizeActionFilter at all (vulnerable build present but feature unused — exposure, not exploitable)
  • Dev/test or internal-only hosts not reachable by untrusted users
  • A 4.3.15+ build whose installer string still carries a pre-release tag that parses to a lower core version
  • Side-by-side install where an old DotVVM copy lingers in a NuGet cache but the running app references the patched assembly
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the flagged SoftwareVersion is the build actually loaded by a running, internet-reachable DotVVM app (not a stale cache copy), and that the app registers AuthorizeActionFilter. 2) Contain: schedule upgrade to 4.3.15 / 4.2.11 / 5.0.0-preview09 (or apply the AuthorizeAttribute workaround) on exposed hosts first. 3) Hunt: pivot each confirmed host into the anonymous-200 IIS hunt (companion UC) to see whether the no-op filter has already been exercised by unauthenticated callers.
DeviceTvmSoftwareInventory
| where SoftwareName has "dotvvm" or SoftwareVendor has "dotvvm" or SoftwareVendor has "riganti"
| extend VerCore = extract(@"^(\d+\.\d+\.\d+)", 1, SoftwareVersion)
| where isnotempty(VerCore)
| extend V = parse_version(VerCore)
| extend PreviewNum = toint(extract(@"preview0*(\d+)", 1, SoftwareVersion))
| where (V < parse_version("4.2.11"))                                                  // whole 4.2.x and earlier line
     or (V >= parse_version("4.3.0") and V < parse_version("4.3.15"))                   // 4.3.0-preview01..4.3.14
     or (V == parse_version("5.0.0") and SoftwareVersion has "preview" and PreviewNum < 9) // 5.0.0-preview01..08
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, DeviceId, SoftwareVendor, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion
| sort by DeviceName asc
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities

| where SoftwareName has "dotvvm" or SoftwareVendor has "dotvvm" or SoftwareVendor has "riganti"
| extend VerCore = extract(@"^(\d+\.\d+\.\d+)", 1, SoftwareVersion)
| where isnotempty(VerCore)
| extend V = parse_version(VerCore)
| extend PreviewNum = toint(extract(@"preview0*(\d+)", 1, SoftwareVersion))

     or (V >= parse_version("4.3.0") and V < parse_version("4.3.15"))

| sort by DeviceName asc

Network-AI sandbox escape: node spawns /bin/sh -c with allowlist prefix + shell metacharacter injection AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the CVE-2026-54051 core exploit: the network-ai SandboxPolicy allowlist glob-matches the whole command string but ShellExecutor runs it via /bin/sh -c, so an allowed prefix (git/npm/node/yarn/pnpm) followed by ;, &&, ||, $() or backticks becomes arbitrary execution. Surfaces a node parent spawning a shell with -c where the command line carries both an allowlisted prefix and an unquoted shell metacharacter. Rationale: Uses the exact mechanism named in the article — node/npm/git/yarn/pnpm allowlist prefixes glob-matched whole-string, executed through /bin/sh -c, plus the article's enumerated metacharacter set (; & | $ ` ( ) < > { }). The node-parent + sh -c + prefix + metachar combination is the article's primary exploit (UC_12_2).
ATT&CKT1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate npm lifecycle scripts that chain shell commands with && (e.g. "build && test")
  • CI/CD runners (Jenkins, GitLab runner) invoking node tooling that shells out
  • pm2 / nodemon process managers launching shell wrappers
  • Developer running a node script that calls git with a $(...) subshell
  • Postinstall hooks combining multiple commands with ;
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm run","npm exec","yarn run","node_modules/.bin") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("&&","||") and not ProcessCommandLine has_any ("id;","whoami","/tmp/","curl ","wget ","nc "))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and the parent node command line; identify whether the shell payload follows a legitimate allowlist prefix and whether the metacharacter introduces an unrelated command (id, curl, nc) vs a benign build chain. 2) Contain: if the host runs network-ai < 5.9.1 and the injected segment is non-build (recon/network), isolate the host and kill the orchestrator process; capture /proc and any dropped marker files. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same node parent for child recon binaries and outbound connections; check for network-ai version and remove broad wildcard allowlist entries (node */npm *).
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node"
| where FileName in~ ("sh","bash","dash")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "-c"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"\b(git|npm|node|yarn|pnpm)\b"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"[;&|`$(){}<>]"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has "node"
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("sh","bash","dash")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "-c"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine matches regex @"\b(git|npm|node|yarn|pnpm)\b"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine matches regex @"[;&|`$(){}<>]"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Network-AI CVE-2026-54051 post-exploit: Linux recon utilities spawned from shell child of node AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects the post-exploitation enumeration that follows a successful CVE-2026-54051 allowlist bypass: a shell (sh/bash/dash) whose parent is node executing reconnaissance binaries. Fires when the orchestrator-spawned shell runs id, whoami, uname, hostname, curl, wget, nc, ncat, python, perl, ssh-keygen or cat. Rationale: Maps the article's post-exploitation profile (id ran via the injected /bin/sh -c in the PoC) and the reviewer-supplied recon binary set, scoped to a node grandparent in Defender/Falcon where InitiatingProcessParentFileName is available. Sentinel ImProcessCreate exposes only parent (sh) so the node grandparent constraint is relaxed there.

  • CI jobs invoking python via a shell step
  • Admin troubleshooting scripts under a node-managed service
  • Container entrypoint shells gathering uname/hostname at startup

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Vulnerable network-ai (<=5.7.1) npm package present in node_modules
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Unauthenticated non-loopback POST /mcp tool invocation against network-ai SSE server
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] network-ai MCP SSE server launched in non-stdio (vulnerable) mode
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Vulnerable network-ai (<=5.7.1) npm package present in node_modules AIDelivery High
Asset-inventory hunt that surfaces hosts with the vulnerable Jovancoding/Network-AI package on disk (CVE-2026-48814 / CVE-2026-46701). The package files land under node_modules\network-ai; presence is the precondition for the unauthenticated MCP SSE exposure. Triage version against the 5.7.2 fixed release. Rationale: Pivots on the concrete install path 'node_modules\network-ai' named in the advisory (bin/mcp-server.ts, lib/mcp-transport-sse.ts live under it). Cache-path exclusions cut npm/pnpm store noise so only real project installs surface for remediation against the 5.7.2 fix. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/cve-2026-48814-cwe-306-missing-authentication-for--a37c283f4afc7554 • https://www.squaredtech.co/mcp-security-flaw-npm-package-ships-with-no-auth-by-default
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer workstation with network-ai intentionally installed for legitimate multi-agent orchestration
  • CI/CD build agent pulling the package during npm/pnpm install for tests
  • Security researcher detonating the package in a sandbox
  • Already-remediated 5.7.2+ install (identical path, patched version — verify package.json version field)
  • Monorepo with nested node_modules copies inflating host count
Suppression filter
| where FolderPath !has @"_cacache" and FolderPath !has @"\.npm\" and FolderPath !has @"\pnpm-store\"
Response runbook
1) Triage: open package.json under the flagged node_modules\network-ai path and read the version; if >=5.7.2 it is patched — close. If <=5.7.1, confirm whether the host runs the SSE MCP server (see companion process UC). 2) Contain: have the owner upgrade to network-ai 5.7.2+, or if the server is running, set a non-empty NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET and rebind the listener to 127.0.0.1 until patched. 3) Hunt: pivot the host into the process-launch and inbound-POST UCs to determine whether the vulnerable SSE server was ever started or reached from a non-loopback source.

network-ai MCP SSE server launched in non-stdio (vulnerable) mode AIInstallation High
Detects process creation of the Network-AI MCP server (command line contains both 'network-ai' and 'mcp-server') without the --stdio flag, i.e. the SSE listener that ships with the empty-default-secret authorization bypass. Stdio-mode invocations are excluded because the advisory's only safe path requires --stdio. Rationale: Uses the advisory's exact invocation tokens — the 'network-ai' package and its 'mcp-server' bin — and excludes --stdio, the only mode the advisory deems non-vulnerable. Surfaces the listening SSE server before exploitation rather than a generic node process. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/ • https://www.squaredtech.co/mcp-security-flaw-npm-package-ships-with-no-auth-by-default
ATT&CKT1059 T1133
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Operator running the server locally bound to 127.0.0.1 for legitimate single-user use
  • Developer testing network-ai during feature work
  • Automated integration-test harness spinning the server up and tearing it down
  • Patched 5.7.2+ host where the secret is now enforced (still launches the same process — confirm version)
  • Container entrypoint launching the SSE server with a properly set NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the on-disk network-ai version (<=5.7.1 = exposed) and whether NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET is set in the service/unit/container env; an empty/unset secret means _isAuthorized() returns true for all callers. 2) Contain: stop the SSE listener or rebind it to loopback, set a strong NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET, and upgrade to 5.7.2+. 3) Hunt: pull DeviceNetworkEvents/web logs for this host and run the inbound-POST /mcp UC to see whether any non-loopback caller already reached config_set / agent_spawn / token_* tools.

Unauthenticated non-loopback POST /mcp tool invocation against network-ai SSE server AIExploitation Medium
Active-exploitation detection for CVE-2026-48814: a non-loopback caller (curl/SSRF/0.0.0.0 bind) issues POST /mcp to the Network-AI SSE server and gets HTTP 200 with no Authorization header, invoking MCP tools (config_set, agent_spawn, blackboard_write, token_*) against the empty default secret. Web/proxy logs carry the path+method+auth-header signal; EDR network telemetry carries the non-loopback inbound exposure. Rationale: Targets the advisory's PoC exactly — POST /mcp, HTTP 200, no auth, non-loopback source — using the web-log auth-header emptiness (ImWebSession.HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization) as the unauthenticated signal, with EDR inbound-non-loopback as a corroborating exposure pivot when proxy logs are absent. Datadog left blank: no generic web-access-log source in this tenant's listed schema carries POST/path/auth-header. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/cve-2026-48814-cwe-306-missing-authentication-for--a37c283f4afc7554 • https://www.squaredtech.co/mcp-security-flaw-npm-package-ships-with-no-auth-by-default
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal vulnerability scanner / ASM probing the /mcp endpoint
  • Authorized local MCP client legitimately sending no Authorization header because the secret was never configured (still the vuln — worth flagging)
  • Reverse-proxy or container health check polling /mcp
  • Container/NAT bridge rewriting a loopback request so it appears as a non-loopback SrcIpAddr
  • Load balancer or service mesh sidecar fronting the SSE server with stripped auth headers
Suppression filter
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the destination host runs network-ai <=5.7.1 SSE mode, then inspect the request URL/body for invoked tool names (config_set, agent_spawn, blackboard_write, token_revoke) and check the source IP against known scanners and internal ranges. 2) Contain: block ingress to the MCP port from non-loopback sources at the host/SG firewall, set a strong NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET, restart the service, and upgrade to 5.7.2+. 3) Hunt: enumerate every POST /mcp 200 from this source across all hosts, review token_* and config_set invocations for tampering or stolen credentials, and rotate any tokens the blackboard/agent tools could have exposed.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "network-ai" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "mcp-server"
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "--stdio"
| where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
| where RemoteIPType != "Loopback" and RemoteIP !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
| summarize Connections=count(), FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), Ports=make_set(LocalPort, 10) by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemoteIPType, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by FirstSeen desc
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where HttpMethod =~ "POST"
| where Url has "/mcp"
| where HttpStatusCode == 200
| where isempty(HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization)
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, HttpMethod, Url, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent=HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

gemini-mcp-tool @file path-traversal / sensitive-file exfiltration via Gemini CLI AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts Gemini CLI invocations (driven by the gemini-mcp-tool MCP server, CVE-2026-0755) whose command line carries an @file reference that escapes the working directory or targets sensitive files (@/etc/passwd, @~/.ssh/id_rsa, @../../secret). Article-specific because it keys on the Gemini CLI @file parser abuse used to read/exfiltrate arbitrary local files through the LLM channel. Rationale: Keys on the article's exact @file PoC strings (@/etc/passwd, @~/.ssh/id_rsa, @../../secret) scoped to a 'gemini' command line — the CVE-2026-0755 exfiltration sink. Cross-checked against the GitLab Advisory DB and Snyk entries which confirm the @file parser as the read/exfil vector. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/gemini-mcp-tool/CVE-2026-0755/ • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-GEMINIMCPTOOL-15091895 • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2026-0755/
ATT&CKT1005 T1552.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer legitimately referencing a sibling project with @../other-repo/file.ts for cross-repo code analysis
  • Power user analyzing their own dotfiles, e.g. gemini -p 'review @~/.bashrc'
  • CI job pointing Gemini CLI at an absolute build-artifact path outside the checkout dir
  • Security researcher reproducing the CVE in an authorized test lab
  • Legitimate analysis of /var/log or /etc config files by an ops engineer using Gemini CLI
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)@\.\./[A-Za-z0-9._-]+/(src|lib|test|tests|docs)/")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and the parent (expect node.exe running the gemini-mcp-tool MCP server). Confirm the @file target is outside the project working directory and is sensitive (passwd, id_rsa, .env, cloud creds). Identify who/what supplied the prompt (MCP client, chat session, automated agent). 2) Contain: kill the MCP server process, upgrade gemini-mcp-tool to >=1.1.6, and rotate any credential file referenced (SSH keys, tokens) since the content was sent to the Gemini API. 3) Hunt: review DeviceNetworkEvents for outbound to the Gemini API from this host around the event, search other hosts for the same gemini @traversal pattern, and check the MCP client logs for the originating untrusted prompt.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "gemini"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)@(\.\.[\\/]|~[\\/]|/(etc|root|home|var|proc)/|[a-zA-Z]:\\)"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "gemini"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)@(\.\.[\\/]|~[\\/]|/(etc|root|home|var|proc)/|[a-zA-Z]:\\)"
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

gemini-mcp-tool Windows cmd.exe metacharacter injection (node -> cmd.exe breakout) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the CVE-2026-0755 Windows command-injection vector: the gemini-mcp-tool MCP server (node.exe) spawns cmd.exe to run the Gemini CLI, and unquoted prompt metacharacters break out of the gemini argument into arbitrary OS commands. Article-specific because it pivots on the node->cmd.exe chain carrying 'gemini' plus shell metacharacters (&, |, >, <, ^, `). Rationale: Pivots on the article's exact Windows injection mechanism — node-spawned cmd.exe running the Gemini CLI with unquoted metacharacters (the 'broken shell:false double-quote wrapping' the 1.1.6 fix removed). The node->cmd.exe->gemini chain is rare and the metacharacter requirement makes it alert-grade; corroborated by Penligent's execAsync sink analysis. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-4h5r-5jm8-jxjm • https://www.penligent.ai/hackinglabs/deep-analysis-of-gemini-mcp-tool-command-injection-cve-2026-0755-when-an-mcp-toolchain-hands-user-input-to-the-shell/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/gemini-mcp-tool/CVE-2026-0755/
ATT&CKT1059.003 T1202
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate prompt that genuinely contains '&' or '|' inside quotes on a patched (>=1.1.6) host where quoting now contains it
  • Developer asking gemini about shell syntax, e.g. a prompt mentioning 'cmd & redirect'
  • Build wrapper that intentionally chains gemini with another command via cmd.exe (&&)
  • Local reproduction of the CVE in an authorized lab
  • A node-based task runner that pipes Gemini CLI output to a file with '>'
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)gemini[^\"]*[&|><^`]"
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the cmd.exe ProcessCommandLine and isolate what follows the metacharacter after the gemini token — that is the injected command. Enumerate child processes of this cmd.exe (DeviceProcessEvents where InitiatingProcessId == this ProcessId) to see what actually executed (whoami, certutil, powershell, net). 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the node MCP server and any spawned children, and upgrade gemini-mcp-tool to >=1.1.6. 3) Hunt: review DeviceNetworkEvents from the host for download/exfil by the injected child, search for the same node->cmd->gemini+metacharacter pattern fleet-wide, and trace the originating untrusted prompt in the MCP client logs.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node.exe"
| where FileName =~ "cmd.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "gemini"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)gemini.*[&|><^`]"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith "\\cmd.exe"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\\node.exe"
| where CommandLine has "gemini"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)gemini.*[&|><^`]"
| where not(TargetUserName endswith "$")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, TargetUserName, NewProcessName, ParentProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["9f1f11a708d393e0a4109ae189bc64f1f3e312653dcf317a2bd406f18ffcc507", "c0ad494457dcd9e964378760fb6aca86a23622045bca851d8f3ab49ec33978fe", "9896a6fcb9bb5ac1ec5297b4a65be3f647589adf7c37b45f3f7466decd6a4a7f", "e60ab99da105ee27ee09ea64ed8eb46d8edc92ee37f039dbc3e2bb9f587a33ba", "2915b3f8b703eb744fc54c81f4a9c67f", "bf9672ec85283fdf002d83662f0b08b7", "38de5b216c33833af710e88f7f64fc98", "dbd8dbecaa80795c135137d69921fdba"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Crawl4AI: Unauthenticated RCE via Chromium launch-argument injection in browser_config.extra_args

ATT&CKT1190
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Chromium subprocess-launcher switch + --no-zygote co-occurrence (Crawl4AI extra_args RCE) · [LLM] Headless Chromium spawning shell/interpreter child (Crawl4AI injected-command exec)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Chromium subprocess-launcher switch + --no-zygote co-occurrence (Crawl4AI extra_args RCE) AIExploitation High
Hunts a single Chromium/headless-shell process whose command line carries --no-zygote together with a subprocess-launcher override (--utility-cmd-prefix / --renderer-cmd-prefix / --gpu-launcher / --browser-subprocess-path). This tuple is the execution signature of GHSA-r253-r9jw-qg44, where attacker-supplied browser_config.extra_args forces Chromium to fork/exec an arbitrary command. The combination is near-pathognomonic — legitimate Crawl4AI never sets a launcher-override prefix. Rationale: Uses the exact launcher-override switches named in the advisory (--utility-cmd-prefix/--renderer-cmd-prefix/--gpu-launcher/--browser-subprocess-path) gated on --no-zygote; per the Chromium zygote docs this combo is what forces a fresh fork/exec of the injected command, making it high-fidelity. Chromium switches are always lowercase canonical so no Datadog casing variants are needed. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/crawl4ai/GHSA-r253-r9jw-qg44/ • https://github.com/chromium/chromium/blob/main/docs/linux/zygote.md
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer debugging Chromium under valgrind/gdb using --renderer-cmd-prefix on a workstation
  • CEF/Electron app that sets --no-zygote for stability but never sets a launcher prefix (won't match — requires both)
  • Automated browser-performance testing harness intentionally wrapping subprocesses
  • Crash-reproduction tooling that prefixes the GPU process launcher
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("valgrind","gdb","--enable-logging=stderr") and InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("bash","sh","make","ctest"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and read the value after the launcher switch — that string IS the attacker command; capture the parent (Crawl4AI/uvicorn/python) and the runtime user. 2) Contain: kill the Chromium process tree, isolate/stop the Crawl4AI container, and rotate any secrets/tokens mounted into it (env, mounted files) since RCE ran as the runtime user. 3) Hunt: confirm the package is <=0.8.9, search for child processes the browser spawned (UC2), and review WAF/proxy logs for the POST /crawl* that delivered the payload (UC3).
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine contains "--no-zygote"
| where ProcessCommandLine contains "--utility-cmd-prefix"
    or ProcessCommandLine contains "--renderer-cmd-prefix"
    or ProcessCommandLine contains "--gpu-launcher"
    or ProcessCommandLine contains "--browser-subprocess-path"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--no-zygote"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--utility-cmd-prefix"
    or ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--renderer-cmd-prefix"
    or ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--gpu-launcher"
    or ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--browser-subprocess-path"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Chromium Subprocess-Launcher Switch With Zygote Disabled (Crawl4AI extra_args RCE)
id: 7b3e1c2a-4d5f-4a6b-8c9d-1e2f3a4b5c6d
description: Detects a Chromium/headless process launched with --no-zygote together with a subprocess-launcher override switch, the execution signature of GHSA-r253-r9jw-qg44 argument-injection RCE in Crawl4AI.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-r253-r9jw-qg44
  - https://github.com/chromium/chromium/blob/main/docs/linux/zygote.md
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/20
tags:
  - attack.t1190
  - attack.t1059.004
logsource:
  category: process_creation
detection:
  selection_zygote:
    CommandLine|contains: '--no-zygote'
  selection_launcher:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - '--utility-cmd-prefix'
      - '--renderer-cmd-prefix'
      - '--gpu-launcher'
      - '--browser-subprocess-path'
  condition: selection_zygote and selection_launcher
falsepositives:
  - Bespoke browser-automation harnesses that deliberately set a launcher prefix for debugging
level: high
source:linux.auditd @process.command_line:*--no-zygote* AND @process.command_line:(*--utility-cmd-prefix* OR *--renderer-cmd-prefix* OR *--gpu-launcher* OR *--browser-subprocess-path*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| regex("--no-zygote", field=CommandLine)
| CommandLine=/--(utility-cmd-prefix|renderer-cmd-prefix|gpu-launcher|browser-subprocess-path)/i
| table([aid, ComputerName, UserName, ImageFileName, ParentBaseFileName, CommandLine])
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.process="*--no-zygote*" AND (Processes.process="*--utility-cmd-prefix*" OR Processes.process="*--renderer-cmd-prefix*" OR Processes.process="*--gpu-launcher*" OR Processes.process="*--browser-subprocess-path*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.process="*--no-zygote*" AND (Processes.process="*--utility-cmd-prefix*" OR Processes.process="*--renderer-cmd-prefix*" OR Processes.process="*--gpu-launcher*" OR Processes.process="*--browser-subprocess-path*") by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.process_name Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
Headless Chromium spawning shell/interpreter child (Crawl4AI injected-command exec) AIExploitation High
Hunts a browser process (chrome/chromium/headless_shell) that forks a shell, network tool or scripting interpreter as a direct child. In normal operation Chromium only spawns more Chromium/crashpad processes; a /bin/sh, curl, python or nc child is the post-exploitation result of the extra_args launcher-prefix injection executing the attacker command as the container runtime user. Rationale: Pivots on the parent→child relationship the advisory describes ('causing Chromium to fork/exec an attacker-controlled command'); in a Crawl4AI container the browser legitimately only spawns more browser/crashpad processes, so a shell/curl/python child is high-fidelity. Datadog left empty because the standard linux.auditd schema exposes no parent-process field to anchor the chain. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/crawl4ai/GHSA-r253-r9jw-qg44/
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1203
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Custom crawler post-processing that legitimately pipes browser output through python/node
  • Container healthcheck scripts mis-parented under the browser during teardown
  • Debug builds invoking curl/wget from a browser test harness
  • Sandbox-disabled CI runs where the browser wrapper is a shell script
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("healthcheck","/usr/local/bin/entrypoint") )
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the child ProcessCommandLine and any network destination it contacted; identify the runtime user and whether the child reached out-of-band (curl/wget/nc to an external IP). 2) Contain: terminate the child + Chromium tree, stop/quarantine the container, and block the egress destination at the firewall; rotate mounted secrets and tokens. 3) Hunt: correlate to UC1 (launcher-switch cmdline) on the same host/time, review FileWritten events by the child for dropped payloads, and check DeviceNetworkEvents for follow-on C2 from the runtime user.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome","chromium","chromium-browser","headless_shell","chrome-headless-shell","chrome.exe","chromium.exe","headless_shell.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("sh","bash","dash","zsh","curl","wget","python","python3","nc","ncat","netcat","perl","ruby","node")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("chrome","chromium","chromium-browser","headless_shell","chrome-headless-shell")

Crawl4AI path-traversal: crawler Python process writes to RCE-enabling paths (cron.d, authorized_keys, shell rc, site-packages) AIInstallation High
Hunts the exploitation primitive of GHSA-2jq4-q6vv-4cp3: the crawl4ai download sink (HTTP Content-Disposition filename or Playwright suggested_filename) escaping the downloads directory and writing attacker-controlled bytes to one of the four RCE sinks the advisory names. A web-crawler Python process should never write to /etc/cron.d, ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, shell rc files, or the Python import path, making this high-fidelity for the file-write-to-RCE escalation. Rationale: Uses the four RCE sinks the advisory explicitly names (/etc/cron.d/evil, ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, shell rc-file, Python module on import path) keyed to a python initiating process, the only attacker-influenced behaviour this file-write-to-RCE bug produces. Cross-checked the GHSA, GitLab GLAD (CWE-22+CWE-59, fix 0.9.0) and VulnCheck's related crawl4ai Docker advisory. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-2jq4-q6vv-4cp3 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/crawl4ai/GHSA-2jq4-q6vv-4cp3/ • https://www.vulncheck.com/advisories/crawl4ai-docker-api-local-file-inclusion-via-file-url-handling
ATT&CKT1190 T1053.003 T1098.004 T1546.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • pip/pip3 or poetry installing or upgrading a package writes legitimately into site-packages/dist-packages during a deploy of the crawler container
  • A maintenance/config-management Python script (Ansible/SaltStack module run via python) legitimately writing a cron job under /etc/cron.d
  • Developer running `python -m venv` or editable installs touching site-packages on a build host
  • Legitimate crawl4ai self-update writing its own package files
  • An ops automation Python tool rotating a service account's ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("pip","pip3","poetry","uv") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("pip install","-m pip","setup.py","python -m venv")) and FolderPath has_any ("/site-packages/","/dist-packages/")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the InitiatingProcessCommandLine and FolderPath/FileName — confirm the writer is the crawl4ai service (uvicorn/gunicorn/python crawler) and not pip/deploy tooling, and inspect the written file's content (cron line, SSH key, rc payload). Check the SHA256 and the source URL/Content-Disposition that triggered the download. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the crawler process, remove the dropped file, and rotate any exposed SSH keys/service creds; confirm crawl4ai is upgraded to >=0.9.0 and CRAWL4AI_API_TOKEN auth is enabled. 3) Hunt: enumerate all files written by that python PID outside the downloads dir, review web/proxy logs for unauthenticated POSTs to the Docker /crawl endpoint with HTTPCrawlerConfig, and run UC2 to confirm whether cron/sshd executed the dropped payload.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has "python" or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "crawl4ai"
| where (FolderPath has_any ("/etc/cron.d","/etc/cron.daily","/etc/cron.hourly","/var/spool/cron","/site-packages/","/dist-packages/"))
    or FileName =~ "authorized_keys"
    or FileName in~ (".bashrc",".bash_profile",".profile",".zshrc",".bash_login")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has "python" or ActingProcessCommandLine has "crawl4ai"
| where (TargetFilePath has_any ("/etc/cron.d","/etc/cron.daily","/etc/cron.hourly","/var/spool/cron","/site-packages/","/dist-packages/"))
    or TargetFileName =~ "authorized_keys"
    or TargetFileName in~ (".bashrc",".bash_profile",".profile",".zshrc",".bash_login")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Crawl4AI Path-Traversal Arbitrary File Write to RCE-Enabling Path
id: 6b1f2c3a-9d8e-4f2b-bc11-3a2e7d4f9c10
description: Detects the crawl4ai download sink (GHSA-2jq4-q6vv-4cp3) writing outside its downloads directory into cron, SSH authorized_keys, shell rc, or the Python import path, the RCE escalation the advisory describes.

author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/20

      - '/.bash_profile'
